## Assistant

## The experiment

Build a small trip workspace and connect it to ChatGPT, so we can test whether a traveler can manage **one shared itinerary** in both places.

The key outcome is not just getting ChatGPT to return trip data. We want to see an **interactive itinerary inside real ChatGPT**, make an intentional change there, and see that same saved change in the standalone app.

## Implementation plan

1. **Build the local trip workspace.** Create a backend and a mobile-usable web view with one synthetic trip day and a few activities. Store the itinerary in a persistent local PostgreSQL database.

2. **Support safe changes.** Let the traveler reorder activities, mark them visited, and edit notes. Add a proposal-and-approval flow so a proposed change does not alter the accepted itinerary until the traveler approves it. Prevent stale edits and duplicate approvals from silently changing the plan.

3. **Connect ChatGPT to the same backend.** Add an MCP integration and an interactive itinerary card that uses the existing trip operations—not a second copy of the itinerary.

4. **Run the real-host experiment.** After checking access and obtaining separate approval for the connection, open the itinerary in ChatGPT, propose and accept a change, and verify it in the web app. Then make a web change and check that ChatGPT sees it too. Test persistence and conflict handling.

## Deliverable

A **working local prototype and evidence report** showing:

- A standalone trip-day web view and an interactive itinerary card rendered in real ChatGPT.
- Changes saved to one shared backend and visible across both surfaces.
- Explicit approval, persistence, and protection against stale or repeated changes.
- Local run instructions, focused tests, and a record of what was actually verified and what remains blocked.

The last saved task state says **nothing has been implemented or tested yet**. The foundation comes first; real ChatGPT testing also depends on account access, a safe connection design, and separate connection approval. The separate question of whether **Free ChatGPT customers** can use a published app remains unresolved; a successful developer test would not answer it.

<skill_content name="manage-project-tasks">
# Skill: manage-project-tasks

# Project Tasks

## Job and boundary

**Make unfinished work portable.** A task is durable execution memory: a shared
human-agent brief that makes planning, investigation, implementation, validation,
or later discussion continuable across sessions, agents, harnesses, and machines.

Use one when the answer to **"Will future continuation of this work need a durable
brief?"** is yes. A task need not be implementation-ready. Its first screen
should help the human understand and supervise the work; a fresh agent should be
able to take the **next permitted action** without the previous conversation.

Use the same contract for engineering, research, organizational approvals, career
work, and other continuing efforts. Adapt evidence and deliverables to the work;
a task does not require code, a repository, or automated tests.

This skill owns the *memory of the work* and its handoff, not the work's implementation method.
Ordinary decision work shapes substantial work when plan review is required;
`tech-design-options` can compare technical approaches when the human requests it.
Execution workflows perform and verify the work; `maintain-project-docs` owns
settled project understanding. Code, tests, schemas, and observed behavior
remain authoritative for their respective implementation claims. Do not create
tasks for ordinary short-lived work with no persistence need.

Saving a task does not approve implementation. Asking to execute a saved task
authorizes only the actions already permitted by the user's request, project
guidance, and task gates. It normally also includes maintaining **that task's
progress and its index entry**, unless stricter guidance requires separate
permission. It does not authorize unrelated task edits, subagents, spending,
deployment, commits, pushes, or documentation/KB writes. Explicit handoff and
closeout requests additionally authorize the bounded persistence review below,
subject to narrower local rules and the human's stated limits.

## Resolve the project and its task surface

Before creating, changing, indexing, or executing a persisted task, read the
applicable `AGENTS.md` task routing and relevant repository guidance. Resolve the
**project from the requested work**, not just the current working directory.
One project can span multiple repositories and use one shared Tasks location;
different projects can share a workspace root.

- Use the configured `Tasks` location. New section-relative paths resolve from
  the declaring AGENTS.md directory; legacy workspace-relative paths retain their
  recorded workspace root. Neither uses the process working directory. Discovering,
  reading, or using relevant task/index context does not require loading this skill.
- During an authorized, human-triggered task-management pass, check the applicable
  `AGENTS.md` section while reading repository guidance. If task routing is
  missing or the owned section is absent or lacks the current discovery and
  maintenance cues, follow [Task routing](references/routing.md) to establish
  or minimally refresh it without a separate request. Skip equivalent guidance;
  never change it merely to read task context or publish a skill. Ask about
  ambiguous identity/ownership; report inaccessible destinations.
- A human can explicitly select a destination for one operation, without
  silently changing the saved map. Create a confirmed but absent directory only
  during an authorized write.
- No guessed `tasks/` fallback, `projects.toml`, or whole-workspace inventory.
  Maintain only the owned Project Tasks section under the routing rules; preserve
  legacy central blocks until an explicitly approved migration.
- The configured task surface (including its `INDEX.md`) is this skill's normal
  writable scope. A verified marked Project Tasks section in `AGENTS.md` may
  also be created/refreshed during authorized management as specified in routing.
  Other guidance, repositories, docs, code, raw evidence, and archives require
  their own authorization/workflows. Report exact physical paths changed, as
  well as useful logical task paths.

Apply narrower repository rules even when this skill would ordinarily maintain
state automatically. In particular, a repository that makes task changes
explicitly opt-in does not gain broader write permission just because execution
started.

## Stable layout and discovery

For **new** tasks, use a creation-month folder and one shallow, semantic
workstream folder inside the configured task surface:

```text
tasks/
  INDEX.md
  YYYY-MM/
    aws-migration/
      YYYY-MM-DD__validate-cutover.md
      YYYY-MM-DD__prepare-rollback.md
    dataset-evaluation/
      YYYY-MM-DD__agree-evaluation-criteria.md
```

`tasks/` here is a *logical* location; its actual root comes from `AGENTS.md`.
Reuse literal workstream names across months; create folders only as needed.
An overview or related-effort subfolder is useful only for independently
continuable tasks sharing meaningful coordination, not for every workstream.
Use descriptive, distinctive filenames. The folder and filename dates are the
task's **creation date**, not the last update, planned execution date, or closure
date. Once created, the path stays stable across Active, Blocked, On Hold, and
Closed. Do not move completed tasks into `archive/` or deferred tasks into
`backlog/`. Git history or the unchanged file preserves historical access.

Legacy task locations, filename suffixes, groups, and gates remain usable.
Read or modify an authorized legacy file **in place**. Do not silently rename,
move, delete, or mass-migrate existing files to the new scheme.

### INDEX.md — the current-work view

Maintain `INDEX.md` automatically as part of every authorized task creation,
material state/priority/next-action change, closure, or reopening. It is a
**navigation view**, not a competing source of truth or a second task record.

- Include links to **all known unfinished tasks**: Active, Blocked, and On Hold.
  Group by `Now`, `Next`, and `Later` priority; show a concise task name, status,
  and meaningful next action or re-entry/blocking condition. Show workstream
  labels so related work remains discoverable across months. Retain a recognizable
  nickname, acronym, or ticket identifier when it helps the human's usual wording
  find the task; use only established, appropriate-to-store terms. Keep this scannable.
- Exclude Closed tasks from the live dashboard. Keep their files at their stable
  paths for filename, month, and targeted-content search. Reopening restores
  the entry. Don't create a separate historical catalogue by default.
- Derive entries from the task files. On routine changes, update only affected
  entries and preserve unrelated content. Check relevant index entries before
  creating a task; use bounded, targeted search when similar work may exist.
- When `INDEX.md` is absent on an authorized write, initialize it from a
  bounded inventory of the configured task surface, including known legacy
  folders. Do not silently present a partial inventory as complete: flag
  unknown coverage if exhaustive discovery is unavailable. Never search other
  projects or rewrite unrelated files to make the index look full.
- Keep relative links and any group overview/child links valid. Avoid redundant
  entries for the same coherent effort; list independently continuable outcomes
  when they need separate tracking. If index maintenance fails, preserve the
  authoritative task write if safe and report the index as out of sync.

No monthly indexes, external registries, background synchronizers, database,
or automatic scheduled sweeps are required. A broader task hygiene sweep is a
separate, explicitly requested operation.

For natural-language continuation such as "where are we with the AWS migration?",
select the relevant project/index, then read the matching task. An absent index
entry is not proof of absence: use bounded filename/content search in the confirmed
task surface, including older months and established aliases. Ask only when
materially different candidates remain plausible; report material access/search
limits. Give a short state/next-action summary; a status question does not authorize
execution. Confirm the relevant working location or external system before acting;
for code work, a shared task folder does not establish the code repository or cwd.

## General maintenance request

When the human generally invokes manage-project-tasks, such as "use manage-project-tasks" or
"manage the tasks", without specifying an operation, start a bounded maintenance
review; do not ask the human to name files or actions. Review the current work
context and relevant saved tasks. Resolve the project and consult its index and
targeted task files; identify all directly
related outcomes, including multiple tasks where appropriate. Decide which existing
tasks need updates, whether a distinct task is needed, whether a task is ready for
closure or resumption, or whether nothing needs changing. Reuse tasks for the same
outcome; ask only when project identity, scope, a materially different match, or a
consequential decision cannot be resolved from available context. Do not expand a
bounded review into an unrelated project-wide hygiene sweep.

- One maintenance request authorizes the agent to independently create, update,
  checkpoint, reprioritize, and close verified-Completed tasks related to the work
  context, with affected index changes. No separate approval for routine task
  bookkeeping is needed. Honor narrower local write gates and user limits; propose
  prohibited changes instead. This permission expires after the pass: do not
  maintain tasks proactively during other conversation or later work.
- Close as Completed only when the required outcome and verification are established.
  Cancelled or Superseded requires clear human intent, not agent inference.
  Explicit closeout still uses the coordinated persistence review; a routine
  Completed closure does not trigger it. Never infer permission to execute work
  from a task's presence, priority, or status.
- Report each meaningful task change with its action, identity, exact physical path,
  and short reason; include affected index paths and any approval/access gaps.
  Report a no-op when nothing changes, and never claim a proposed change was made.
  Routine maintenance does not authorize execution or docs, KB, or lessons writes.

## Task depth — Brief / Standard / Detailed

**Standard is the default.** Select the level by the requested handoff and its
consequences, not a word-count quota. Respect an explicit mode; use Detailed
when the human specifically wants a strong planner to prepare work for a smaller
executor. Do not silently inflate routine tasks.

When authoring or substantially revising a task, show **one compact human-facing
line** identifying the selected mode and alternatives, for example:

`Project Tasks · Standard (also: Brief / Detailed)`

This is output observability, not mandatory task metadata or a heading in the
saved file. Omit the mode line for ordinary reading, execution, or minor status
updates.

- **Brief:** preserve an idea, early investigation, or lightweight handoff.
  Capture goal/why, what's known or undecided, permission boundary, and next
  meaningful action. Compactness does not excuse ambiguity.
- **Standard:** a fresh-session working brief. Add success bar, current state,
  selected direction or explicit uncertainty, important constraints, blockers,
  verification requirements, and an actionable next step.
- **Detailed:** a high-fidelity planner-to-executor handoff. Preserve consequential
  decisions and rationale; the chosen architecture or strategy; grounded files,
  interfaces, and dependencies where known; ordered phases and checkpoints;
  acceptance tests or observable outcomes; important edge cases, recovery paths,
  stop conditions, and boundaries of executor discretion. Clearly distinguish
  observed facts, planned changes, assumptions, and unresolved human choices.
  Include the details a smaller model might otherwise have to rediscover.

**Trust the executor's ability to implement, but do not require it to
rediscover decisions that the planner has already resolved.** Detailed does
not mean micromanaging every command or prescribing speculative code edits.
Correctness, honesty, and executable clarity apply at all depths. Preserve
useful depth through handoff; compress completed or superseded mechanics as
work proceeds without discarding still-binding decisions, acceptance criteria,
or important failure lessons.

### Optional outcome discovery

When a substantial task is being prepared for execution and its goal or success
bar has consequential uncertainty, **offer** a define-success discussion with
the human. Invoke that skill only if the human accepts; do not convert task
creation into a mandatory discovery session. When an agreed definition already
exists, preserve its success conditions and unresolved choices faithfully
rather than rediscovering them. A Brief task may capture an early idea before
its success bar is settled.

## Task contract

Keep the first screen useful before any long plan. A normal new task has:

```markdown
# Task: [Clear outcome]

**File:** `tasks/YYYY-MM/<workstream>/YYYY-MM-DD__descriptive-name.md`
**Created:** YYYY-MM-DD
**Last Updated:** YYYY-MM-DD
**Priority:** Now | Next | Later
**Status:** Active | Blocked | On Hold | Closed

**Goal:** [...]
**Why:** [...]
**Deliverables:** [concrete outputs or changes, when useful]
**Success Bar:** [...]
**Chosen Direction:** [selected | not yet selected]
**Current State:** [...]
**Next Action:** [action AND whether it is permitted]
**Blockers:** [none | blocker, impact, and clearing condition]
```

Combine `Why` with `Goal` or omit empty optional fields on simple tasks. Add
`Human Attention`, `Permission Boundary`, `Verification`, `Docs Sync`,
`Relevant Code`, or deeper sections only when they improve control or
continuation. Do not turn this outline into a mandatory form.

The goal describes the intended outcome; deliverables name reviewable outputs
(for example a decision brief, an approval outcome, a validation report, or a PR); the success bar establishes their
acceptance. Combine these on small tasks rather than requiring three empty fields.

The human should quickly know **what/why, what is decided, what is true now,
what is allowed next, what blocks it, and how success will be verified**.

Use two independent dimensions:

- **Priority:** Now = current focus; Next = intended next-up; Later = deferred
  but worth retaining. Later replaces a separate backlog folder.
- **Status:** Active = progress intended; Blocked = next meaningful action
  prevented by a dependency or human decision; On Hold = intentionally paused;
  Closed = work ended, not necessarily successfully implemented.

Closing may mean **Completed**, **Cancelled**, or **Superseded**. Record the
reason and actual verification/outcome when consequential; never portray
cancelled work as implemented. Closure does not change the file path. A paused
or blocked task needs a useful re-entry condition. Priority and status are
*not* execution permission.

### Decisions, plans, and uncertainty

For material choices, record one of: **locked/approved decision**, **executor-owned
routine detail**, or **unresolved choice with a permitted investigation or human
decision**. Preserve consequential product, architecture, security, privacy,
migration, fallback, cost, and user-behavior choices. Don't hide them behind
"either", "optionally", or "choose during implementation".

A task should have one current direction and current state; it need not pretend
the direction is selected before it is. Keep it self-contained for a fresh
session, linking canonical sources rather than copying competing project truth.
Rework stale sections instead of appending a session diary. Preserve history only
where it prevents a recurring mistake or explains a still-binding constraint.

At a material checkpoint, reconcile the affected task's header, body, checklists,
resume commands, and index entry. Replace obsolete next actions; remove redundant
instructions or label retained plans as historical and non-actionable. An appended
outcome does not cancel an earlier instruction on its own. Preserve useful evidence,
rationale, and unfulfilled acceptance criteria; do not turn this into a historical
purge or a sweep of unrelated tasks.

Useful optional Detailed sections: `Decisions Locked`, `Grounded Current
Understanding`, `Implementation Strategy`, `Execution Phases`, `Acceptance and
Verification`, `Edge Cases and Recovery`, `Stop Conditions`, `Executor
Discretion`, and `Context Pointers`. Use only those that earn their space.

When substantial planning requires review, present the plan and wait for approval.
Persist an approved plan without re-requesting ceremonial approval; do not
execute future investigations or preflight checks merely to write the task.

### Resume context

On creation, resumption, or substantial handoff, consult relevant project docs and use KB
recall when prior findings could materially inform the work. Read-only retrieval
does not authorize source maintenance. Keep a small context map with exact paths
or remote locators, what each source supplies, and when it must be read. Identify
approved design, observed evidence, procedures, and unresolved investigation
accurately. Match verification to the claim: an official policy, decision record,
source artifact, or observed result may be relevant; code/runtime controls current
implementation claims. Capture time and applicability when they affect reuse.

```markdown
## Resume Context
- Before the next action: <docs path>#<heading>
  — approved constraints and rationale.
- Before validation: <KB locator>
  — observed limitation, scope/version, and remaining uncertainty.
- Working location: <confirmed workspace, system, or code repository>.
- Dependency: <task path> — consumes its verified compatibility report.
```

Use real sources only; omit inapplicable entries. Retain consequential constraints
and the next action in the task itself so the human can supervise it. Verify
pointers and consult required sources before dependent work; an inaccessible
required source is a specific readiness gap, not permission to guess. Conditional
references wait until relevant. Reconcile material conflicts with current evidence.

## Resume, execute, checkpoint, and close

### Coordinated handoff and closeout

In project-task context, "hand this off to a fresh session", "prepare/delegate
the next tasks to another agent", and "we're done; close this task" request a
coordinated persistence review. No slash commands or special word "full" are
required. Read [Handoff and closeout](references/handoff.md): evaluate KB capture,
docs integration, and operational lessons through their owning skills, verify
supporting material, then save task state/context and index navigation. Each
review may correctly produce no change. Respect a narrower "task only" request.

This does not spawn agents or approve implementation/publication. Routine status
updates and ordinary execution bookkeeping do not trigger the cross-surface review.

### Pause for a fresh session

Treat cues such as "let's pause", "continue in a fresh session", or "pick this
up next time" as a request to save a **fresh-session continuation handoff** when
the conversation concerns unfinished project work. If a task already exists,
update that task in place; otherwise create one in the configured task surface
when authorized. Preserve the goal, decisions and rationale already settled,
current progress and verification, unresolved questions, blockers, relevant
context pointers, and an unambiguous next permitted action. Make the brief
self-contained without the present chat. Update `INDEX.md` accordingly and
report the exact task path plus a short resume cue.

An explicit fresh-session handoff also uses the coordinated workflow above;
a simple progress checkpoint need not. Save consequential decisions at meaningful
checkpoints while context is available, rather than relying on a final transcript
reconstruction after context has been lost.

A conversational pause does **not** imply implementation approval, execution
preflight, or that the work is intentionally deferred. Keep `Status: Active`
when work simply continues in a later session; use `On Hold` only when the human
intentionally suspends the work pending a meaningful re-entry condition. Do not
start additional investigation or implementation merely to finish the handoff,
and do not silently create a task when the user is merely ending casual chat.
Honor configured locations and narrower write permissions.

When asked to **execute work from a saved task**, load this skill and the task,
identify its configured project/task surface, read applicable `AGENTS.md` guidance,
and inspect the relevant current sources, docs, and evidence, including code when
applicable. Reading or using saved
task context alone, including answering a question about it, does not require
loading this skill or authorize task writes.
The task is execution intent and memory, **not proof that the actual work,
external state, or environment still matches it**.

1. **Check authority and readiness.** Read the status, current direction,
   explicit permission/gates, blockers, next action, dependencies, and success
   bar and deliverables. Read the task's required resume context before dependent
   work. Check later plans and resume commands against the current outcome; do not
   repeat completed work because an old imperative survives. Resolve contradictions
   using relevant evidence, asking when intent or permission remains unclear; a
   recent timestamp alone does not settle a conflict. Distinguish preparing a handoff
   from permission to implement. Do not treat an index entry as authorization.
2. **Act within the approved scope.** Perform only the next permitted work through
   the relevant execution workflow. If current evidence contradicts the
   plan or creates a material human-owned decision, stop and surface it rather
   than silently redesigning, spending, deploying, or escalating access.
3. **Maintain execution memory automatically when authorized.** At meaningful
   checkpoints, blocked transitions, handoffs, and completion, update the same
   task's current state, decisions affected, blockers, verification actually
   performed, material deviations, remaining uncertainty, and next action;
   refresh its index entry. Do not record every command or trivial progress.
   If a stricter rule withholds task-write permission, report what needs an
   update instead of editing.
4. **Close honestly.** Close when the required outcome and verification are
   complete, or the human explicitly cancels/supersedes the remaining work.
   For an explicit closeout request, complete the coordinated review first.
   Record the result and closure reason, distinguish tested from untested,
   and identify residual follow-up. If required work remains, leave the task
   open or, when authorized, make a distinct follow-up task. Remove only the
   closed task's index entry; do not archive, delete, or relocate its file.
5. **Report succinctly.** State what changed, verification and gaps, any
   consequential plan deviation or human action, current task status, and
   exact task/index paths updated. A failed or partial execution still needs
   an accurate checkpoint when writes are allowed.

"Execute this task" ordinarily includes the bookkeeping above; the human
should not need to separately ask to update, close, or re-index it. This
does not override narrower repository write rules or permission gates.

### Execution gates and operational preflight

Read legacy `Execution Gate` fields conservatively. Honor prohibitions and
human unblocks; `Waiting for Dependency` needs evidence before proceeding;
`Ready for Main-Agent Execution` permits only its stated action;
`Ready for Delegated Execution` indicates suitability, not permission to
spawn subagents; `Not Applicable (Closed)` permits none. Missing or old
metadata is not implicit authorization. Preserve material legacy gates when
touching older files; do not mass-migrate them.

Apply operational preflight **relative to the next action** when preparing
immediate execution or performing risky activity, including investigations
that access production, sensitive data, or paid services. Check what matters:
approval and access, private-data handling, cost/quota, external dependencies,
interfaces/schemas, production state, destructive effects, backups/rollback,
retries, observability, and a safe verification path. Do not demand later-phase
preflight just to checkpoint an idea.

For a blocker, state what is blocked, why, what will clear it, and the evidence
of clearance if non-obvious. Distinguish human unblock from ordinary dependency.
Never poll indefinitely, work around a human gate, or mark READY for a blocked
next action. A safe investigation may proceed while implementation is blocked
if its own permission and prerequisites are satisfied.

Before claiming execution readiness or closure, check consistency among
status, priority, direction, blockers, permission, next action, and success
bar/verification. A `Closed` task with required unperformed work, `Blockers:
None` alongside an actual blocker, or an "approved" decision contradicted by
the plan is not a valid handoff.

Never store secrets, keys, passwords, tokens, or recovery codes in task files.
Minimize sensitive or restricted data; link approved protected sources instead.

## Related tasks, knowledge, and maintenance

Prefer one file per coherent outcome, even with several steps. Use the
creation-month/workstream related-effort group only when its children have
independently continuable outcomes. `overview.md` owns common intent,
dependencies, decisions, and suggested entry point; children own their own
state and verification. Numeric prefixes express sequence, not readiness.
Keep the index navigable without duplicating every child dashboard.

Before creating another task, inspect the index and targeted relevant existing
tasks. Reuse or update an existing task when the outcome is the same; link
related-but-distinct work and dependencies instead of creating competing plans.
Do not combine genuinely independently continuable outcomes just to reduce
file count.

Route settled project knowledge to `maintain-project-docs` when authorized; a task may
record that docs sync remains required without silently writing docs. KB capture
requires a capture request or the explicit coordinated handoff/closeout intent
above; ordinary task maintenance does not authorize it. A broader sweep to identify
duplicate, stale, abandoned, or inconsistent tasks requires a specific request; ordinary writes
only repair related entries and direct conflicts.

**Final test:** A human can supervise the current work, a fresh agent can
continue the next *permitted* action without the old conversation, and the
index points to the actual unfinished tasks without moving or duplicating
their authoritative files.

Read file docs/product.md, lines 1-47
1: ---
2: title: Tripverge Product Direction
3: description: Product intent, human control, and travel workflow invariants for Tripverge.
4: updated: 2026-11-19
5: ---
6: 
7: # Product Direction
8: 
9: Tripverge is an intended commercial travel workspace for individuals and groups to turn travel research into a shared, dependable trip record and use it throughout a trip. A polished mobile-first app and compatible AI assistants work with the same durable, structured trip state; conversations help people work with that state rather than replacing it.
10: 
11: The initial customer hypothesis is a person organizing a sufficiently complex trip for a partner, family, or friends, while keeping the product useful for solo travelers. Companions may have individual as well as shared arrangements. The desired experience helps people enjoy shaping a trip and feel in control without imposing a bureaucratic workflow. Demand, willingness to pay, and the right group experience remain to be validated.
12: 
13: The differentiator to test is a dependable, evidence-aware travel workflow: decisions, bookings and accessible supporting evidence, review status, unresolved tasks, meaningful approval, and safe change handling. When a traveler asks what was booked, the product should make the basis and uncertainty of the answer understandable. A stored or linked confirmation is evidence, not automatic verification that a reservation is still valid.
14: 
15: First-class use through travelers' preferred capable assistants, including ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini where supported, is a core product bet: Tripverge provides the trip record and travel workflow rather than trying to recreate a general-purpose assistant. Interactive embedded UI is a related bet to validate. MCP connectivity alone is not a differentiator. The standalone website is also a first-class product, not a chat transcript viewer. Host capabilities and interoperability must be checked individually, not assumed.
16: 
17: Users can create, approve, edit, reorder, and review trips themselves, while an agent can interpret, research, propose, and guide work. Manual assembly is possible but not the assumed default. Both surfaces must obey the same product rules and use the same authoritative state.
18: 
19: ### Notes and manual interaction
20: 
21: Travelers should edit familiar fields, not learn Markdown syntax. An activity has a title and narrative notes; users can write the notes themselves or ask an assistant to draft changes to that same field. Present a simple formatted editor with ordinary text, bullet lists, bold and links, backed by a limited Markdown representation. The PWA and supported MCP Apps interface render that content as formatted text. Raw Markdown editing is not required.
22: 
23: Keep the supported formatting consistent across generation, editing, saving and rendering so reopening a note does not silently discard formatting. Do not execute raw HTML or permit unsafe links. Notes are untrusted user/source content, not instructions that can authorize agent actions. Exact editor/renderer libraries remain implementation choices.
24: 
25: Plan selection, approval of a revision, a supplier booking, evidence review and a traveler-reported visit are distinct facts. Marking an activity visited records the traveler's report; it does not independently verify attendance, payment or an external reservation. An optional idea must not become a selected commitment merely because it appears in narrative guidance.
26: 
27: ## Workflow invariants
28: 
29: - Persist who approved which revision and when. Approval means the same thing across clients.
30: - An activity and its booking have independent states. Moving an activity never silently changes an external reservation.
31: - Attaching a document or link, finding a confirmation, and reporting payment do not by themselves verify a booking. Track evidence source, what was checked, review status, and remaining uncertainty.
32: - Distinguish fixed commitments from flexible suggestions. Flag conflicts rather than silently overwriting paid or fixed arrangements.
33: - Reject or reconcile approval of a stale proposal; never silently overwrite a newer revision. Meaningful changes should be inspectable and reversible where appropriate.
34: - Treat booking evidence and travel information as sensitive. Restrict access by user and trip, use least privilege and time-limited file access, and do not expose booking identifiers or ticket URLs in public output. A Gmail link is accessible only to someone with appropriate account access.
35: - If subscription limits are introduced, enforce them server-side. Pricing and feature gates are not approved.
36: 
37: ## Direction and limits
38: 
39: The intended experience is a responsive, mobile-first PWA and useful controlled widgets inside supporting AI hosts. Support for other hosts is an aspiration, not a claim of universal UI, authentication, or file interoperability. Offline access requires a separate design; installability does not provide it automatically.
40: 
41: Usable access for Free ChatGPT customers is a critical commercial assumption for the founder, not an acceptable-to-ignore plan restriction. Distinguish permission to connect an unpublished custom MCP server for development from a customer's ability to discover, connect to and use a published app. Verify connection, read/write actions and embedded UI separately on relevant plans, regions and surfaces. A successful paid/developer-account experiment does not establish broad customer availability. Evidence that Free customers cannot use the essential journey requires a human-led reassessment; it does not authorize a paid-only pivot or automatic substitution of the standalone website as the answer.
42: 
43: The commercial hypothesis is that travelers organizing sufficiently complex trips will choose, and some will pay for, a clearer and more dependable way to manage plans, bookings, evidence, and changes while using their preferred assistants. Revisit this hypothesis and the product bets using proportionate customer, competitor, technology, cost, pricing, and distribution evidence at meaningful milestones. These are hypotheses, not proven moats or permission to make consequential changes without human approval.
44: 
45: The Japan planning material that inspired this product is private reference material, not content to publish or import automatically. The first paid promise, distribution, collaboration model, and in-trip experience remain to be validated. The working tagline is not final brand approval.
46: 
47: See [MVP](mvp.md) for the first outcome and [Architecture](architecture.md) for system boundaries.

1: ---
2: title: Tripverge Intended Architecture
3: description: Intended system boundaries, technology choices, data contracts, and security constraints.
4: updated: 2026-11-19
5: ---
6: 
7: # Intended Architecture
8: 
9: These are approved directions, including the local-experiment decisions confirmed on 19 November 2026, not a description of an implemented or deployed system.
10: 
11: ```text
12: Compatible agents and widgets             Mobile-first web / PWA
13:           | MCP                                   | HTTP API
14:           +------------------+--------------------+
15:                              |
16:                 Shared FastAPI domain operations
17:                              |
18:                Supabase PostgreSQL + private Azure Blob files
19: ```
20: 
21: The website and agent-facing server must invoke the same authorized, validated domain operations. Database state is canonical, not chat history, widget state, static HTML, or a separate Markdown itinerary. Import/export is not a second write authority. Stable trip/day/activity deep links must open the intended view subject to authentication.
22: 
23: ## Selected technology direction
24: 
25: | Concern | Direction |
26: | --- | --- |
27: | Code and delivery | GitHub monorepo and GitHub Actions. Repository privacy is intended, but remote privacy has not been verified here. |
28: | Web | Next.js, React, and TypeScript; mobile-first responsive PWA, hosted on Vercel. |
29: | Domain API | Python FastAPI, packaged with Docker for local development and eventual Azure Container Apps hosting; FastAPI provides the OpenAPI contract. |
30: | Python dependencies | uv for development, CI and production image builds, with a committed project lockfile. Install dependencies at image build time, not dynamically at application startup. |
31: | Azure infrastructure | Bicep for future Azure provisioning; deployment remains separately authorized. This does not imply that Bicep provisions Supabase or Vercel. |
32: | Agent access | FastMCP calling the same business operations as the HTTP API. |
33: | Embedded UI | Controlled React `DayItinerary` delivered through MCP Apps. Follow current official MCP Apps and OpenAI guidance; prefer the standard UI bridge and use ChatGPT-specific extensions only where needed. |
34: | Database and identity | Supabase PostgreSQL and Supabase Auth, initially Google sign-in. |
35: | Local experiment database | PostgreSQL in Docker with a persistent volume and synthetic data. This defers cloud hosting, not durable application state. |
36: | Private files | Azure Blob Storage, with metadata and access control in the backend/database. |
37: 
38: FastAPI's OpenAPI contract should drive generated TypeScript types and/or a client, avoiding duplicate handwritten contracts. Generation and compatibility checks belong in CI; they do not replace runtime validation or API versioning. Keep domain logic out of Vercel-only functions unless a strong reason emerges.
39: 
40: ## Trip records and narrative notes
41: 
42: Store actionable trip state as structured relational records with stable identities, not as a whole Markdown itinerary that clients independently rewrite. Markdown remains useful inside an activity's description/notes field; it is not a second source of truth. See [Product](product.md#notes-and-manual-interaction) for the editing experience.
43: 
44: PostgreSQL remains the selected primary store. Having dated activities does not by itself justify a time-series database: the main workload is related, editable trip records, decisions and permissions, not high-frequency measurements. Time zones, approximate or unset times, activity order, proposal revisions, approval attribution and history need deliberate domain/schema/API design. Full event sourcing, a separate time-series store and generalized undo are not selected for the experiment. Exact schemas, transaction boundaries and any audit/revert representation remain design work.
45: 
46: Use compatible PostgreSQL migrations and explicit timezone semantics locally so that the slice can move to the intended Supabase deployment without silently changing its meaning. A local database does not establish Supabase Auth, row-level security, backup/recovery or production compatibility. Keep files outside database narrative fields; synthetic evidence metadata is sufficient for the experiment.
47: 
48: For uv-based Docker builds, pin the toolchain, enforce the lockfile, separate dependency installation from frequently changing source layers, and use appropriate build caching. uv need not remain in the final runtime image. Do not copy a host virtual environment into the container.
49: 
50: ## MCP Apps interaction and local connection
51: 
52: MCP tools and MCP Apps are complementary: tools return structured results and perform operations; a UI-enabled tool references a server-provided HTML/JavaScript resource that a compatible host renders in a sandboxed frame. Our React widget presents the returned itinerary rather than asking the traveler to inspect JSON. Its controls can call permitted tools through the host bridge without requiring another model inference for each click.
53: 
54: All widget writes must pass the same backend authorization, approval and revision checks as website writes. Database state remains authoritative; the widget's displayed state can be stale. Render only an appropriately scoped result, never unrestricted database rows or private proof credentials.
55: 
56: The approved development approach is a local Docker-backed service reached through OpenAI Secure MCP Tunnel, subject to actual account access and action-specific connection approval. The tunnel client must reach the local MCP service and maintain its outbound connection. A private tunnel is not a public product deployment, a substitute for user authorization, or proof of Free-plan access to a published app. Azure provisioning is unnecessary for this local experiment.
57: 
58: Current implementation references, to recheck before integration:
59: 
60: - [OpenAI: add UI to an MCP server](https://developers.openai.com/plugins/build/chatgpt-ui) — UI resources, standard bridge, state ownership and rendering guidance.
61: - [MCP Apps](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/extensions/apps) — protocol and host capability model.
62: - [OpenAI Secure MCP Tunnel](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/secure-mcp-tunnels) — local connectivity and separate organization/workspace permissions.
63: - [uv in Docker](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/guides/integration/docker/) — lockfile-based image builds and caching.
64: 
65: ## Security and interoperability
66: 
67: Website login alone does not define authorization for remote MCP clients: OAuth consent, scopes, and agent permissions need their own design. Validate identity and authorization for every mutation, including direct widget interactions that do not require another model inference. Enforce tenant isolation and private file access at the backend; use time-limited access and never expose public ticket URLs.
68: 
69: Test each supported host's rendering, authorization, approval, and file capabilities; provide text or deep-link fallback where appropriate. PWA caching, offline sensitive-document protection, upload safety, email-linked evidence, image rights, maps quotas, operational costs, and observability remain open design work. No provider, host support, offline behavior, or deployment has been verified by this document.
70: 
71: The conceptual entities include trips, days, activities, revisions, approvals, bookings, proofs, todos, users, and trip membership. They are a design sketch, not an approved database schema. See [Product](product.md) for domain invariants and [MVP](mvp.md) for the initial acceptance boundary.

1: ---
2: title: Tripverge First MVP
3: description: Intended first product slice, acceptance criteria, and material unresolved design questions.
4: updated: 2026-11-19
5: ---
6: 
7: # First MVP
8: 
9: Prove one durable trip across the standalone app and an agent host, ideally with a real trip, rather than building a general travel platform first. Any Japan-plan import requires an explicit privacy-aware decision; do not publish private booking proofs in a demo.
10: 
11: ## First local experiment
12: 
13: The approved first experiment is narrower than the full MVP: **show one persistent synthetic trip day in an interactive MCP Apps itinerary inside ChatGPT, make an intentional change through it, and observe the same saved state in the standalone web app.** Use the local stack in [Architecture](architecture.md); cloud provisioning and private-trip import are not prerequisites.
14: 
15: The experiment should answer:
16: 
17: 1. **Invocation:** Does real ChatGPT call our Tripverge tools and retrieve current backend state? Record the actual account plan, host surface and test date.
18: 2. **Model fidelity:** Can the day distinguish selected/flexible activities, optional alternatives, fixed booking details and unverified evidence without turning narrative suggestions into commitments?
19: 3. **Intentional change:** Can the user inspect an assistant's proposed change and explicitly accept it, with approval tied to the exact revision and the accepted change visible in the website?
20: 4. **Shared state:** Do manual reorder, traveler-reported visited status and supported activity notes survive reload/service restart and appear on a fresh assistant fetch without relying on chat memory?
21: 5. **Safe failure:** Are stale proposals rejected rather than silently applied, repeated acceptance handled without duplicate changes, and saved/conflict outcomes reported honestly? An itinerary edit must never imply a supplier booking change.
22: 6. **Useful embedded interaction:** Does the actual React `DayItinerary` render inside ChatGPT, display formatted notes, support the bounded actions above and show the backend's saved/conflict result? Can the founder follow what changed without reconciling separate plans?
23: 
24: A tool-only/JSON-only connection and MCP Inspector checks are intermediate technical checkpoints, not completion of the embedded-UI experiment. Text and authenticated deep links remain useful fallbacks, but unavailable host UI must be reported as a blocked or unverified part of the outcome, not silently substituted as success. Initial founder usability feedback is not proof of broader demand or willingness to pay.
25: 
26: Free-plan access to a published app is a separate distribution validation track. Record its evidence independently of the private tunnel result; do not assume one proves the other. A comparable published-app test is indicative, not definitive proof of Tripverge's own eventual availability.
27: 
28: Use one synthetic day, a few activities and synthetic booking/evidence metadata. Defer real files, supplier writes, general undo, preference management, collaboration invitations, full production identity/operations and cloud CI/CD deployment. The experiment still needs a safe, explicitly scoped access/actor design before connecting the local service to a real host; deferring production identity is not permission to expose an unrestricted development mode.
29: 
30: ## Intended slice
31: 
32: 1. Establish a mobile-first web app, domain API, database, and authentication with secrets kept separate.
33: 2. Persist and display a trip/day and structured activities with durable deep links.
34: 3. Expose agent tools for retrieving and proposing or editing a day through the same domain operations.
35: 4. Provide one controlled `DayItinerary` MCP Apps widget to show items, reorder, approve, and show saved status; the standalone website supports the same operations. Activity notes follow the [formatted-editing contract](product.md#notes-and-manual-interaction), and traveler-reported visited state is separate from approval and booking status.
36: 5. Demonstrate cross-surface approval and editing with current revisions and stale-write detection.
37: 6. Add private booking evidence metadata, including a PDF or link, review status, and outstanding issues. Attached is not verified.
38: 7. Test mobile touch use, small screens, authorization, errors, and privacy/access boundaries.
39: 
40: **Acceptance:** two independently navigable clients share one durable plan without reliance on chat memory; approvals and versions are correct; the experience is usable on a phone; and evidence metadata and access are controlled.
41: 
42: ## Decisions requiring design or validation
43: 
44: - Domain schema, state transitions, proposed changes, revision conflict semantics, and audit trail.
45: - Remote MCP authorization, consent and scopes alongside Supabase Auth; host registration and actual MCP Apps support.
46: - Shared UI packaging, widget sizing/sandbox behavior, and the division between inline and full-app editing.
47: - Secure uploads and content safety, evidence links and expiry, PWA offline caching, and sensitive-data safeguards.
48: - Product discovery and paid promise, plus hosting plans, resource limits, observability, and running costs.
49: 
50: Defer route optimization, real-time flight ingestion, broad marketplace features, native apps, automatic Gmail import, automated photo sourcing, booking purchases, detailed monetization, generalized multi-agent orchestration, and sophisticated in-trip operations. Image and navigation enrichment remain desired but provider and licensing decisions are deferred.

1: ---
2: title: Tripverge Trip Records and Markdown Notes
3: description: Why dated trips remain relational records, how Markdown supports narrative notes and what history/undo still requires.
4: created: 2026-11-19
5: updated: 2026-11-19
6: ---
7: 
8: # Trip records and Markdown notes
9: 
10: ## What was learned and approved
11: 
12: The founder approved structured records with Markdown descriptions/notes for the first iteration. Familiar editing controls should hide Markdown syntax: ordinary title text and a formatted Notes area with bullets, bold and links. The assistant and a human editor update the corresponding activity field, not competing copies of an itinerary. Both the PWA and MCP Apps interface render that field as formatted text. A limited formatting subset, rather than arbitrary Markdown/HTML, was explicitly accepted; editor and renderer libraries were not selected.
13: 
14: This resolves a real distinction in the private Japan planning references: narrative guides explain reasons, alternatives and practical cautions, while actions such as approving, reordering and marking visited need stable record identities and explicit state. Only these generic workflow patterns were retained; no private itinerary, traveler details, booking identifiers or proof links were imported into this repository or approved for demo use.
15: 
16: The canonical product contract is [Notes and manual interaction](../../../../docs/product.md#notes-and-manual-interaction). The primary-store direction and open modeling work belong in [Architecture](../../../../docs/architecture.md#trip-records-and-narrative-notes).
17: 
18: ## Why PostgreSQL remains the direction
19: 
20: A schedule contains dates, but is not necessarily a time-series workload. Tripverge primarily needs relationships among editable activities, trips, travelers, supplier commitments, evidence and decisions. A measurement store's time-bucket aggregation and high-ingestion advantages do not by themselves resolve these needs. PostgreSQL remains the selected primary store; no separate time-series database was selected.
21: 
22: The important modeling distinction is between what a traveler intends, what a supplier has reserved, what evidence supports a claim, what a person approved and what the person reports having visited. A universal `confirmed` flag would collapse different meanings. Local time and timezone context, optional/unset timing, stable activity IDs and revision checks need deliberate design; the database choice is not the design itself.
23: 
24: History, undo and preferences can be represented in PostgreSQL, but their product rules are not automatic. An undo must not silently erase a later edit or imply cancellation of an external booking. Full event sourcing, generalized undo and a preferences subsystem were not approved experiment requirements. The discussed current-state-plus-history approach remains a modeling recommendation, not an approved detailed schema.
25: 
26: Supabase documentation read in the conversation says `timescaledb` is unavailable from PostgreSQL 17 onward. Do not plan around legacy extension availability without checking the actual target version. This reinforces the need to distinguish a database capability from a provider's supported configuration; it is not a benchmark or proof that a dedicated time-series service could never be useful.
27: 
28: ## Formatting and trust boundaries
29: 
30: - Keep generated, edited and rendered content within the same supported subset; round-trip tests should catch silent formatting loss.
31: - Treat Markdown as data, not executable HTML or authority for an agent action. Validate links and render safely.
32: - Store business status in structured fields, not prose that an LLM must reinterpret on each read.
33: - Markdown export can be a derived human-readable view; it is not a second canonical write path.
34: 
35: ## Source basis
36: 
37: - Source: founder approval and discussion on 19 November 2026; no editor, schema, persistence or undo implementation was tested.
38: - Private managed Japan guides were read as contextual examples with permission. Their content was not copied; they remain private source material, not the authority for Tripverge's schema.
39: - [PostgreSQL date/time documentation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/datatype-datetime.html), read during the conversation: timezone-aware timestamps retain an instant, not the original named timezone.
40: - [PostgreSQL range types](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/rangetypes.html), read during the conversation: range/overlap support is a mechanism, not a decision to reject all overlapping activities.
41: - [Supabase: migrating from TimescaleDB](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/migrating-to-pg-partman), read during the conversation: provider/version extension constraints.

7: 
8: # Tripverge local MCP Apps experiment
9: 
10: ## What was agreed
11: 
12: In the 19 November planning conversation, the founder approved showing one persistent synthetic trip day in an interactive MCP Apps itinerary inside ChatGPT, making an intentional change through it, and observing the same saved state in the standalone web app. This is the first experiment, not a production launch or a model-API experiment hosted by Tripverge.
13: 
14: The approved direction uses FastAPI, FastMCP, Docker and uv; local PostgreSQL in Docker has a persistent volume. GitHub Actions is the selected CI/CD direction, and Bicep is selected for eventual Azure provisioning. The reason to use local infrastructure now is to defer cloud deployment and account/cost setup, not to defer persistent state. The canonical current direction lives in [Architecture](../../../../docs/architecture.md); the experiment outcome lives in [MVP](../../../../docs/mvp.md#first-local-experiment).
15: 
16: Approving the plan did not authorize creating accounts, configuring a tunnel, provisioning infrastructure, spending, publishing, committing, pushing or spawning agents. The final request authorized KB/docs/task persistence and delegation into future task briefs, not execution of those briefs.
17: 
18: ## How the interface fits the tools
19: 
20: MCP tools still return structured results and perform backend operations. MCP Apps adds server-provided UI resources and a host bridge so a compatible host can render an interactive HTML/JavaScript interface inside the conversation. For Tripverge that interface is a controlled React `DayItinerary`, not raw JSON displayed to travelers.
21: 
22: The intended interaction is: ask for the day; fetch current records; render the itinerary through an MCP Apps UI resource; use a bounded control such as Approve or Mark visited; validate and persist through the same domain operations as the PWA; display the authoritative returned result. A direct widget action need not require another model inference, but it still needs server authorization and revision checks.
23: 
24: The agent initially emphasized a tools-only path too strongly. The founder corrected this because the embedded itinerary was already part of the intended product. Tools-only checks remain implementation checkpoints; they are not an approved replacement for the experiment's MCP Apps outcome. If actual host UI is unavailable, retain that blocked/unverified result instead of redefining success.
25: 
26: ## What remains untested
27: 
28: No application, database, image, tool call, widget rendering, host identity mapping or tunnel connection was implemented or exercised in this conversation. Local Docker/PostgreSQL prerequisites and the founder's actual ChatGPT/Platform permissions were not verified. The repository was observed to contain planning material rather than application code; its Git remote points to GitHub, but remote privacy was not checked.
29: 
30: The planned connection is OpenAI Secure MCP Tunnel from the local environment, subject to real access and separate configuration approval. Its outbound connection avoids a public inbound listener for the MCP server, but does not make arbitrary local development endpoints safe or satisfy public app submission requirements.
31: 
32: The experiment should expose model fidelity, proposal approval, shared persistence, stale-write and duplicate-request handling, honest booking/evidence distinctions and initial founder usability. It does not prove public availability, production security or commercial demand. [Free-plan access](2026-11-19--chatgpt-free-plan-app-access.md) needs separate evidence.
33: 
34: ## Sources and continuation
35: 
36: - Source: founder decisions and corrections in the 19 November 2026 Tripverge planning conversation; implementation was explicitly deferred to future tasks.
37: - [OpenAI: add UI to your MCP server](https://developers.openai.com/plugins/build/chatgpt-ui), read during the conversation: standard MCP Apps resources/bridge, server-owned business state and UI behavior. Recheck at implementation time.
38: - [MCP Apps overview](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/extensions/apps), read during the conversation: tool/UI-resource relationship, sandboxed presentation and host-dependent capabilities.
39: - [OpenAI Secure MCP Tunnel](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/secure-mcp-tunnels), read during the conversation: local connectivity, separate Platform/ChatGPT permissions and distribution limits.
40: - [uv in Docker](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/guides/integration/docker/), read during the conversation: pinned uv, lockfiles, cached dependency layers and optional removal of uv from the runtime image. Merely installing uv does not guarantee every image rebuild is faster.
41: - [Task index](../../../../tasks/INDEX.md) — current work and permission boundaries; this record is retained reasoning, not execution authority.

<skill_content name="manage-python-env">
# Skill: manage-python-env

## What I do

I manage Python environments strictly using the modern `uv` project API. I prevent environment drift by exclusively using `pyproject.toml` and `uv.lock`. I **never** use standard `pip`, `uv pip`, `requirements.txt`, or manual virtual environment activation (e.g., `source .venv/bin/activate`).

## How to use

**1. Adding & Upgrading Dependencies**
* **Add package:** `uv add <package>` (Automatically resolves, installs, and updates `pyproject.toml` / `uv.lock`).
* **Add dev dependency:** `uv add --dev <package>` (e.g., `pytest`, `ruff`).
* **Upgrade package:** `uv add --upgrade <package>`
* **Custom Index (e.g., PyTorch):** `uv add torch torchvision --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu124`

**2. Removing Dependencies**
* **Remove package:** `uv remove <package>` (Removes from environment and config files).

**3. Executing Code**
* **Run scripts/apps:** Always prefix execution with `uv run` to ensure isolation.
    * *Example:* `uv run python main.py`
    * *Example:* `uv run pytest`

**4. Syncing & CI/CD**
* **Manual Sync:** Run `uv sync` only if `pyproject.toml` was modified manually or after pulling upstream changes. (Note: `uv add` handles syncing automatically).
* **Strict Sync (CI/CD):** Run `uv sync --locked` to strictly install from `uv.lock` without updating it. Fails if the lockfile is out of sync.

**5. Debugging**
* **View Dependencies:** Run `uv tree` to see the exact hierarchical dependency tree of the current project. Do **not** use `pip freeze`.

**6. Version Control Context**
* Ensure `.venv/` is in `.gitignore`.
* Ensure `pyproject.toml` and `uv.lock` are tracked in version control.

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\tanch\.claude\skills
Relative paths in this skill (e.g., scripts/, reference/) are relative to this base directory.

<skill_content name="run-and-manage-python">
# Skill: run-and-manage-python

# Manage Python Environments

Own both environment management AND utilization. Before the first Python command,
including interpreter checks and import probes, load this skill and select
or create the right purpose project, then actually execute through it. Do not just
inventory environments or recommend one while running elsewhere.

## Resolve and select

1. Read `Python Environment Root` in applicable `AGENTS.md`. New section-relative
   paths resolve from its declaring directory; legacy workspace-relative paths
   retain their recorded base, never the shell's current directory. For missing
   routing or an authorized routing change, read [Python routing](references/routing.md).
   This skill can establish its own resources without workspace-setup. An explicit
   destination selected by the human for this operation is allowed without changing
   the saved routing. Report inaccessible configured resources and ask about
   conflicting ownership/destinations. No `projects.toml` or separate environment registry.
2. Identify the workload's code root and Python/dependency constraints. It may be
   a repository nested below the workspace; environment storage need not be in Git.
   Read project instructions and team dependency files without changing them.
3. Inspect immediate purpose folders under the configured root. Use one independent
   uv project per purpose, directly under that root, e.g. `offline-eval` or
   `data-analysis`. Reuse one when its Python and dependencies are compatible,
   including across repositories. Add missing purpose-related dependencies there.
   Split only for a distinct purpose or incompatible requirements; use meaningful
   names such as `retrieval-eval-py311`, not dates, agent names, or random IDs.
4. Treat environments as shared across sessions. Before any command that can sync,
   modify, or rebuild one, establish that no conflicting run is using it. Even
   `uv run --locked` may sync. Coordinate or stop if uncertain; do not invent a
   locking service or clone an environment merely to avoid waiting.
5. Resolve paths and require the selected purpose project to stay inside the
   configured root. Refuse symlink/junction/reparse redirection, unexplained existing
   directories, and unknown bare virtualenvs. Do not adopt or overwrite them.

Reading a path or source file is not Python execution. Selecting a repository's
bootstrap script does not select or authorize its repository-local environment.

Use `<env>` below for the absolute purpose-project path and `<code>` for the
workload's code root. Paths are separate on purpose. The context home can be a
Git repository, part of one, or not versioned at all.

## Boundaries

- Never create, use, sync, or change a team/root `.venv` unless explicitly requested
  for this task. Its existence or a root `uv.lock` does not grant permission.
- Leave team manifests, locks, pins, requirements, deployment settings and tracked
  `.gitignore` unchanged unless modification is explicitly in scope.
- No `uv run --with`, `--isolated`, `uvx`, `uv tool install`, bare `uv venv`,
  activation, pip, `uv pip` patches, or system-Python workload fallback.
- Follow approved package-index, certificate, proxy, and interpreter-download
  policy. Never bypass policy or change machine-wide settings.
- uv caches, managed interpreter installations, and temporary build environments
  may live outside the root; the purpose project and its `.venv` must not.
- A virtualenv isolates dependencies, not code's filesystem or network permissions.
- Do not auto-prune environments. Removal needs an explicit request.

## Preserve the recipe; exclude generated state

A purpose folder contains:

```text
<env>/
  pyproject.toml
  uv.lock
  .python-version
  .venv/
```

The first three files are reproducible configuration and may be versioned; do not
ignore the whole purpose project. `.venv`, Python caches, and secret files must not
be committed. Never copy credentials into a manifest, lockfile, or report.

Before creating generated files, determine the actual Git worktree containing
`<env>` if any. In an explicitly personal context repository, inspect existing
ignore rules and add narrowly scoped exclusions for `.venv/`, `__pycache__/`,
`*.pyc`, `.env`, and identified secret files inside the configured Python area.
Use its tracked `.gitignore` so exclusions survive recovery. Preserve existing
rules, check `git ls-files` for already tracked generated/secret paths, and verify
with `git check-ignore -v` (not a negating rule). If generated files are already
tracked, stop rather than untracking them automatically. Never stage or commit
as incidental environment management.

If the context is inside a team worktree instead, do not change its tracked ignore
file: use exact context-relative exclusions in its Git-local `info/exclude`,
resolved with `git rev-parse --git-path info/exclude`. If ownership is unclear, ask.
Without Git, no Git setup is required; report that backup is not established rather
than initializing a repository. Inspect output/status to ensure team configuration
remains unchanged. Secret exclusion patterns are not a substitute for data review.

## Target every uv operation

Check `uv --version`; if uv or a required option is unavailable, stop rather than
silently upgrading or switching tools. Use native uv commands, no custom manager.
Use the current shell's syntax and quoted absolute paths.

For each project operation, explicitly select `<env>`. Scope
`UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT` to `<env>/.venv` for those commands only. Clear inherited
`VIRTUAL_ENV`, `PYTHONHOME`, and `PYTHONPATH` in that command scope; inspect conflicting
uv overrides such as `UV_PROJECT`, `UV_PYTHON`, and `UV_NO_SYNC` and remove them
only in that scope. Restore prior values afterward. Preserve approved proxy/index/
certificate settings. Never use `--active`.

### New purpose project

Choose Python from the workload requirement, then a compatible code pin, then an
installed supported stable Python. Resolve an exact version and pin it. Acquire
an unavailable interpreter with `uv python install` only if organizational policy
permits; otherwise ask. No silent Python upgrades on reuse.

```text
uv init --bare --no-package --no-workspace --vcs none --python <version> "<env>"
```

Before add/lock/sync/run, put this boundary in the new local `pyproject.toml`:

```toml
[tool.uv.workspace]
```

It makes the purpose project independent from parent uv workspaces. Do not enroll
members or edit a parent's workspace. Set `requires-python` to the workload's
actual supported range when needed; a pin alone does not narrow that range.

```text
uv python pin --project "<env>" <version>
uv lock --project "<env>"
uv sync --project "<env>" --locked
```

### Existing purpose project

Inspect the manifest, pin, lockfile, and independent workspace boundary before
mutation. Add a missing empty boundary only to a known agent-owned standalone
project; do not overwrite an existing workspace configuration. Reuse its recorded
Python version. If a pin is absent, recover the exact version from the environment
before syncing; ask if it cannot be established safely. Restore an absent `.venv`
with locked sync. Inspect missing/stale locks before deliberately relocking; do
not regenerate a lock merely to bypass an error.

## Manage dependencies AND execute

| Need | Command |
| --- | --- |
| Add a required dependency | `uv add --project "<env>" <package>` |
| Inspect dependencies | `uv tree --project "<env>" --locked` |
| Restore from the lock | `uv sync --project "<env>" --locked` |
| Intentionally upgrade one package | `uv lock --project "<env>" --upgrade-package <package>`, then locked sync |
| Remove an explicitly unwanted dependency | `uv remove --project "<env>" <package>` |
| Execute a script | `uv run --project "<env>" --locked python "<code>/scripts/evaluate.py"` |
| Execute tests | `uv run --project "<env>" --locked python -m pytest` |

Autonomously add dependencies necessary for the authorized purpose; `uv add` and
`uv remove` already update manifest/lock and sync. Ask before incompatible changes,
Python changes, or removing dependencies other work may need. No blanket upgrades
or preinstalled generic tool suites. Never hand-edit lockfiles.

`--project` selects the environment, NOT the process working directory. Run from
the workload's code root unless its instructions require otherwise, using absolute
script/input/output paths as needed. Do not change into `<env>` to run repository
code. An explicitly needed local package can be installed with
`uv add --project "<env>" --editable "<code>"`; inspect build instructions and
possible source-tree build artifacts. Do not convert an unpackaged repo or migrate
its package manager. Inspect requirements before importing them with
`uv add --project "<env>" -r "<requirements>"`.

Invoke scripts through `python`, not `uv run script.py`: PEP 723 inline metadata
can select a separate script environment. Carry those requirements into the
purpose project instead of editing the shared script. Use a scoped `--env-file`
only when required; do not copy its secret values into configuration or reports.

## Verify, recover, report

Before the workload, with the same scoped targeting, run:

```text
uv run --project "<env>" --locked python -c "import sys; print(sys.executable); print(sys.prefix); print(sys.version)"
```

Confirm executable and prefix belong to `<env>/.venv` (Windows:
`.venv/Scripts/python.exe`; POSIX: `.venv/bin/python`). Stop on mismatch. Then
run the real workload or relevant entry-point/import check through that project.
Inspect Git status/diffs where applicable for unintended shared configuration edits.

Diagnose errors using the selected project's pin, dependencies, lock and command.
Never fall back to root/system Python, delete the lock, relax constraints blindly,
or bypass package policy. Rebuild a broken `.venv` only when known owned and idle,
preserving the recipe. Report purpose, created/reused, absolute project/interpreter
paths, Python version, lock path, dependency changes, and actual workload result.
Inventory-only requests list purpose folders, pins, direct dependencies, and
lock/interpreter presence, without claiming runtime verification or writing a
second registry. Runtime use does not require rewriting AGENTS.md routing.

## Explicit team/root mode

Only an explicit human request selects the code repository's standard environment
for that task. Explain the switch and inspect the setup. Explicit targeting and
verification still apply, but do not insert the agent workspace boundary into team
configuration. Prefer locked operations for existing uv projects. Initializing,
migrating, modifying dependencies/locks/pins still needs its own authorization.
For an explicitly requested standalone root virtualenv, `uv venv` is permitted;
do not invent a root uv project. Return to configured purpose projects for unrelated
work. This exception is not permission to bypass missing workspace configuration.

<skill_content name="fastapi-practices">
# Skill: fastapi-practices

# FastAPI Practices

## Job and limits
Make a bounded FastAPI change fit the project's version, public contract, and runtime behavior. Use this during actual FastAPI route, dependency, and schema implementation/review. Do not install packages, change framework versions, move files, or replace the project's chosen ORM/environment solely because an upstream example prefers another tool. Applicable project instructions and existing working conventions take precedence.

## Procedure
1. Inspect the installed/version-pinned FastAPI and Pydantic versions, nearby endpoints, models, tests, OpenAPI generation, and actual run commands. Consult [FastAPI's official skill](https://github.com/fastapi/fastapi/blob/master/fastapi/.agents/skills/fastapi/SKILL.md) or version-matched docs only for patterns relevant to this change. If unavailable, mark version-sensitive assumptions.
2. Define explicit input and output models; separate request fields from internal records and returned fields. Use response validation/serialization that avoids exposing secrets or cross-user data. Keep intentional status codes, including validation and conflict responses, stable.
3. Prefer modern typed parameter/dependency declarations supported by the project's version. Reuse authentication/database dependencies; do not duplicate policy logic in each route. Keep authorization on server-side operations, not just frontend controls.
4. Choose sync or async handlers based on the actual called libraries. Do not block the event loop with synchronous I/O inside an async handler. Manage database transactions and resource cleanup at a clear owner; avoid check-then-write races for revisions or ownership.
5. Inspect the resulting OpenAPI contract and affected callers when modifying externally consumed endpoints. For app/agent clients sharing operations, preserve the same backend rules and predictable error semantics.
6. Execute the smallest relevant tests or runtime checks permitted by the task; add focused checks for newly consequential behavior and report commands, results, and unverified paths. Do not claim a contract or deployment works because types compile.

## Stop condition
The changed endpoint follows the existing project and version's patterns, its relevant data/security boundaries and contract are accounted for, and concrete checks or limits are reported. No blanket refactor, arbitrary test quota, or infrastructure setup.

## Sources and evidence
Based on [FastAPI's official skill](https://github.com/fastapi/fastapi/blob/master/fastapi/.agents/skills/fastapi/SKILL.md). The 2026 SWE-Skills-Bench reported positive average results for a FastAPI skill in its tested configurations; that is not a guarantee for this adapted text or another agent.

<skill_content name="react-nextjs-practices">
# Skill: react-nextjs-practices

# React and Next.js Practices

## Job and boundary
Improve correctness and understandable performance of an actual React/Next.js change. First match the project's React/Next.js versions, routing mode, server/client boundaries, and existing conventions. Do not treat performance advice as proof of a bottleneck or apply all rules mechanically. Avoid framework upgrades, dependency additions, cache policy changes, and broad refactors unless requested or required for the agreed change.

## Focused decision sequence
1. Identify the user-visible behavior, data sources, sensitive values, and components/routes touched. Preserve the app's canonical backend state and avoid showing optimistic success for rejected mutations.
2. Prevent *material* sequential request waterfalls where independent calls can run together. Avoid fetching unnecessary data or duplicating requests; prefer simpler existing project patterns to new abstractions.
3. Choose client and server execution intentionally. Keep private data out of shared caches, static payloads, or unauthenticated client output. Validate backend permission regardless of frontend rendering choices.
4. Keep state ownership clear: derive render state where possible; avoid effects that merely copy props or create update loops. Split components for meaningful boundaries, not to satisfy a code-size target.
5. Look for expensive dependencies or avoidable re-renders only when the change introduces credible cost or observed regressions. Prefer measuring before optimizations with complexity or caching trade-offs.
6. Exercise affected interactions and loading/error states. Use accessible, responsive behavior, then run the existing build/type/test checks relevant to the change and report observed results.

## References
Use the relevant part of [Vercel's React Best Practices](https://github.com/vercel-labs/agent-skills/tree/main/skills/react-best-practices), which groups guidance by waterfalls, bundles, server and client behavior, re-renders, and rendering. Its broad rule list is a lookup resource, not a checklist to exhaust. For visual direction use `frontend-design`; for a dedicated UI audit use `web-interface-review`.

<skill_content name="security-hardening">
# Skill: security-hardening

# Security Hardening

## Job and authority
Protect the specific trust boundaries touched by the task. Security review does not authorize probing real user accounts, external systems, production, paid services, changing access controls, adding monitoring that records sensitive data, or deploying. Ask before high-impact security changes outside the approved implementation scope.

## Risk-focused procedure
1. Name the asset, actor, action, and trust boundary. Trace one normal path and one plausible misuse path (e.g., a member of Trip A accesses Trip B's proof, or an AI tool performs an unapproved mutation). Prefer concrete abuse cases over a generic exhaustive checklist.
2. Inspect existing identity, session, tenant ownership, authorization, validation, data access, file storage, logs, and tool boundaries as applicable. Treat browser checks, UI visibility, and an LLM's instruction as insufficient authorization for backend actions.
3. Apply the smallest effective controls at the authoritative boundary: reject unauthorized reads and writes; constrain accepted inputs; keep secrets out of responses, logs and client bundles; use limited-lifetime access for private files when appropriate; guard server-side fetches against unintended destinations; define approval and revision checks for agent changes.
4. Check dependency advisories, supply-chain hazards, and security headers only when relevant to affected packages or surfaces. Avoid blind updates and broad configuration changes.
5. Exercise representative denial/failure paths using synthetic identities/data and authorized test environments. A happy-path test, screenshot, or static scan alone cannot certify security. Record observed results and remaining risks; recommend specialist review for consequential unresolved exposure.

## Stop condition
The identified boundary has explicit expected behavior, relevant controls and executable checks where possible, and clear residual risk. Do not claim security certification or implement an all-OWASP audit by default.

## Source
Risk-focused adaptation of [Addy Osmani's Security and Hardening](https://github.com/addyosmani/agent-skills/tree/main/skills/security-and-hardening) and established web trust-boundary practices.
