<skill_content name="delegate-implementation">
# Skill: delegate-implementation

# Delegate Implementation

## Purpose and non-negotiables

**Move useful, clearly bounded implementation work to lower-cost workers while the main agent remains accountable for the result.** This is not an instruction to spawn subagents. Main-agent execution is the default until delegation has a credible advantage.

- **One human approval per bounded delegation plan**, not one conversational approval per subagent. The approved plan may contain several named workers; launch only those workers, within their authorized model, task, scope, permissions, and limits. Respect any unavoidable harness permission prompts. Silence, previous task approval, or a user's general enthusiasm for delegation is not plan approval.
- **A genuinely cheaper worker model is a hard prerequisite.** Determine the main agent's model where possible; confirm the proposed worker is actually available in this harness, will run under an explicitly selected lower-cost model, and can execute the needed tools. Do **not** rely on subagents inheriting the parent model. If this cannot be established, **do not delegate, even when requested**. Do not invent model identifiers, pricing, subscriptions, or quota access.
- Suggest a model the human is likely to recognize: **DeepSeek or Luna when available and appropriate**. These are preferences, not assumed provider/model IDs or guaranteed prices. State the exact configured model identity if known; otherwise explain the missing information. The user may change the proposed model before approving.
- **Resource economy is part of the decision.** Cheaper per token does not ensure lower total cost. Factor in extra context, coordination, verification and retries. Use a conservative initial worker count (often one), limited attempts, and no speculative swarms. If a credible saving or work-quality benefit cannot be articulated, keep the main agent.
- **No hidden authority expansion.** An approval to delegate does not authorize an unapproved feature, extra files, branch creation, credential exposure, security changes, cloud costs, publication, deployment, force push, merge, or destructive action. Respect narrower repo, harness and human gates.

## 1. Decide whether any delegation is worthwhile

Use the existing user request, accepted design/contracts, repository state, and (when present) relevant Project Task. A large or long-running task by itself does **not** warrant subagents. The goal is *implementation delegation*, not handing all early exploration to scouts: the user typically investigates goals and design with the main agent first.

Prefer a bounded **implementation worker** when:
- desired behavior and success criteria are already sufficiently clear;
- one meaningful slice can be described with a stable interface, concrete outputs, and a coherent test boundary;
- its write scope can be separated from current parent/other-worker edits or executed sequentially without collision;
- the chosen cheaper worker is capable of the slice and the main agent can independently inspect and integrate it;
- expected saved main-model effort plausibly exceeds coordination, integration and retry overhead.

**Choose no subagents** for straightforward edits, tightly coupled changes that demand continuous shared context, ambiguous product decisions, unsettled API contracts, active Git conflicts, insecure or unapproved access, critical security/transaction invariants best retained by the main agent, weak verification paths, or insufficient cost/model/isolation evidence. Do not ask permission merely to decline; continue ordinary authorized execution.

Read-only exploration, alternative attempts and independent review are optional *supporting* delegation patterns, not the default or a compulsory prelude. Use only when they make the approved implementation materially more reliable or tractable. Avoid redundant research that the human/main agent already completed.

## 2. Propose a single approval package

If delegation passes the gates, present a concise proposal **before any child is launched**:

- **Why:** what the main agent would otherwise do, why this particular bounded implementation is better delegated, and the no-delegation fallback.
- **Workers:** number and purpose (default to one); proposed **explicit provider/model ID** when actually discoverable, why it is cheaper than the main model, and any pricing/quota uncertainty. Do not silently choose a different model.
- **Work contract for each:** deliverable, agreed behavior/constraints, exact or bounded files/modules, interfaces, permitted edits/tools, required checks, and stop/escalation conditions.
- **Coordination:** isolated workspace/worktree or sequential/nonoverlapping write protocol, parent-owned integration, maximum initial attempts and any available budget limits. Distinguish cost estimates from observed usage.
- **Approval boundary:** specify that one approval covers **all listed workers and their bounded actions**. Ask once: “Approve this delegation plan?” Do not ask the same question again for each child.

If essential runtime/model facts are missing, **report the blocker instead of seeking approval for a plan the harness cannot execute**. Do not create or modify harness agent configuration, obtain paid access, or install a provider unless separately authorized. A materially different worker model, expanded scope, additional unplanned workers, extra spending, or changed permissions **is not covered** by the old approval: stop and obtain approval for a revised plan rather than quietly escalating.

## 3. Execute the approved worker plan

1. **Recheck reality:** approved request, model routing, tool permissions, worktree/branch/dirty state, file ownership and isolation. A separate chat/session does **not** imply a separate filesystem. In OpenCode, inspect actual agents/tools/config; model inheritance is not evidence of a cheaper worker. In another harness use its actual supported equivalents.
2. **Isolate writers:** prefer a single worker on a bounded change with the main agent avoiding its files until handoff. For multiple concurrent *mutating* workers, require actual independent workspaces/worktrees or an equivalent verified isolation strategy, distinct write sets and a safe integration path. Where Git/worktree operations are governed by `git-branch-to-review`, follow that workflow and its separate gates; do not manufacture branches here. Without safe isolation, run one writer sequentially or decline delegation.
3. **Give self-contained briefs:** state required objective, relevant existing decisions, exact inputs/outputs/contracts, allowed paths, test commands if known, forbidden actions, and when to stop. Do not transmit the entire conversation or secrets. Workers should report actual changed paths, checks/output, errors, blockers and anything left incomplete. No recursive delegation by default.
4. **Observe without micromanaging:** allow the approved workers to complete their bounded work without repeated human approval or noisy progress requests. Avoid duplicate main-agent implementation of the same slice. Check meaningful results before additional planned waves; keep retries bounded and based on new evidence.
5. **Integrate with main-agent ownership:** inspect the real diff/files against baseline and the accepted contract, reconcile interactions and tests, check combined behavior and significant failure cases, and report what is verified versus merely claimed. A worker saying “done” is not evidence. Reject/repair in-scope defects through the approved process; do not silently launch replacement workers or upgrade to expensive models.
6. **Checkpoint using existing owners:** when executing a saved Project Task, use `manage-project-tasks` for authorized progress, current revision, verification state and next permitted action. When delivering managed branch/PR work, use `git-branch-to-review`. These are *conditional integrations*, not prerequisites for a standalone request. Do not create a second task ledger, worker registry, or mandatory report.

## 4. Stop, account, and learn

Stop delegation on worker-model mismatch, unavailable low-cost execution, exceeded agreed limits, unsafe concurrent writes, ambiguous side effects, repeated failure without a fresh hypothesis, or permission/scope drift. Preserve authorized work, state what remains local versus committed/published, and return control to the main-agent path when permitted.

A short receipt should identify **delegated vs retained work, actual worker model(s), observed usage if available (otherwise unknown), integrated changes, checks and gaps, and remaining human decisions**. Do not claim token savings from a hypothetical price or independence from a reviewer who saw the same context.

**Completion means the approved user outcome is supported by integration evidence, not that child sessions finished.** The skill earns its place when a suitable worker meaningfully reduces main-agent effort or improves outcomes with acceptable resource use; choosing no delegation should remain cheap and routine.

## Boundaries and sources

This is a cross-harness behavioral workflow with OpenCode as its first likely consumer. It requires actual accessible subagent/model tooling to execute and creates none. It is **not** a long-lived queue, background job service, autonomous multi-agent team, or substitute for human approval. For OpenCode-specific current model/permission details consult [OpenCode agents](https://opencode.ai/docs/agents) when needed; do not hardcode version-specific tool names.

Related Agent Core owners: `manage-project-tasks` (continuity), `git-branch-to-review` (Git delivery), `code-review-quality` and applicable checks (review), `define-success` (explicit outcome exploration). This skill does not automatically invoke those workflows for unrelated work.

Research motivation: [When Sub-Agents Work in Parallel (2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10263) describes both selective long-horizon benefit and regressions/overhead, supporting a strong **no-delegation** option and explicit resource gates; these are not measured results for this Agent Core implementation.

<skill_content name="frontend-design">
# Skill: frontend-design

# Frontend Design

## Job and limits
Produce a coherent visual experience that fits the actual product, users, and purpose rather than a generic generated template. Respect approved brand, accessibility, product scope, content, and design constraints. Do not invent product features, replace genuine data with misleading mock facts, or require a redesign when a focused UI adjustment is requested.

## Design and build
1. Identify the screen's primary user decision or action, the real content it must communicate, its use context (including small screens), and existing design conventions. For a new direction, propose one coherent visual treatment rather than an unsolicited moodboard of alternatives.
2. Make hierarchy carry meaning: distinguish committed versus tentative states, primary versus secondary actions, editable versus locked information, and saved versus pending/failed actions. Choose typography, spacing, color, and imagery based on this content rather than fashion or generic SaaS patterns.
3. Design for representative content lengths and real failure/empty/loading states. Preserve legibility, sufficient contrast, focus indication, touch usability, and reduced-motion preferences. Motion must communicate an interaction or state, not merely decorate.
4. Implement or describe the smallest coherent scope authorized by the request. Reuse existing components/tokens where sensible; change broader patterns only when the identified design goal needs it.
5. Inspect the actual UI in a supported renderer when available, including a phone-sized viewport. Distinguish a screenshot/design critique from actual usability or accessibility verification; report untested conditions.

## Stop condition
The requested screen/flow has an explainable visual hierarchy grounded in real content, and the result or design recommendation accounts for consequential states and constraints. Do not create an identity system or full component library without a product need.

## Source
Adapted from [Anthropic Frontend Design](https://github.com/anthropics/skills/tree/main/skills/frontend-design), with an explicit preference for product constraints over prescribed aesthetics.

4: configuration is missing, routing must change, or the applicable managed section
5: is absent or missing current cues. No separate request to maintain that section
6: is needed during the pass. This skill owns only
7: `<!-- BEGIN PROJECT TASKS -->` through `<!-- END PROJECT TASKS -->`
8: in the verified applicable AGENTS.md. A workspace is the human's working scope,
9: not necessarily cwd or a Git root. Ask if scope/project identity is ambiguous.
10: 
11: 1. Use established configuration or the human's operation-specific destination;
12:    the latter does not silently change saved routing. Preserve explicit shared
13:    cross-project task surfaces without inventing a project.
14: 2. During authorized task management, inspect/reuse an unambiguous existing
15:    conventional resource and register missing routing without a second adoption
16:    approval. Confirm identity/ownership from guidance and contents, not its name.
17:    Read-only inspection is not permission to register or initialize anything.
18: 3. If no suitable resource exists, propose
19:    `.work-context/projects/<project>/tasks/` under the confirmed workspace and
20:    obtain one approval for initialization and registration. Established paths,
21:    including repository-owned `tasks/`, remain valid. Initialize only task resources.
22: 4. Report inaccessible configured resources. Preserve narrower write gates and
23:    ask about conflicting ownership/destinations; never create a competing root.
24: 
25: ## Section
26: 
27: Creation-time example only; substitute confirmed paths and a useful description:
28: 
29: ```markdown
30: <!-- BEGIN PROJECT TASKS -->
31: ## Project Tasks
32: When saved task context is relevant, consult the task index and matching tasks.
33: For an imperfect reference or index miss, search filenames/content in the confirmed
34: task root across months using likely subjects, known nicknames, or ticket identifiers.
35: Ask when materially different matches remain; disclose material access/search limits.
36: Read the actual task: reconcile its current state, outcome, and next action before
37: following any older plan or resume command. Verify consequential claims against
38: relevant evidence; unresolved intent or permission conflicts require clarification.
39: Reading or using task context does not require manage-project-tasks or authorize writes
40: or execution. If no match is found, continue within the current request and permissions.
41: 
42: Load manage-project-tasks for task maintenance or execution of a saved task.
43: Task maintenance is human-triggered. When requested, independently create,
44: update, checkpoint, reprioritize, and close verified-Completed tasks for relevant
45: work, maintaining INDEX.md without separate routine bookkeeping approval.
46: Report meaningful changes and reasons, or no-op. Authorization ends with that
47: pass; do not maintain tasks during unrelated conversation. Do not infer
48: cancellation, implementation, deployment, or docs/KB/lessons permission.
49: Stricter local write gates and human limits prevail.
50: 
51: ### Forecasting
52: Tasks: .work-context/projects/forecasting/tasks/
53: - [Task index](.work-context/projects/forecasting/tasks/INDEX.md)
54:   — unfinished forecast reconciliation and rollout work.
55: <!-- END PROJECT TASKS -->
56: ```
57: 
58: New relative paths resolve from the directory containing the declaring AGENTS.md.
59: Legacy workspace-relative paths keep their recorded base until approved migration.
60: Keep unfinished-work navigation in INDEX.md and execution state in task files;
61: routine progress does not rewrite this section. Initialize index coverage honestly
62: under the main skill's rules; never advertise a nonexistent index as saved.
63: Keep the discovery and current-state cues usable without loading the maintenance
64: skill. Put recognizable workstream terms in index entries rather than growing a
65: second task catalogue in AGENTS.md. Verify affected paths/links during authorized
66: maintenance; report unavailable targets rather than silently substituting roots.
67: 
68: ## Targeted maintenance
69: 
70: Markers/headings/labels and the initial activation cue are defaults. During each
71: authorized task-management pass, reuse the applicable AGENTS.md already read for
72: routing: if the owned section is absent, or lacks an equivalent read-side discovery
73: cue or human-triggered maintenance authorization, create or minimally patch it
74: without a separate request. Skip a sound section without rewriting it; do not
75: perform a broad workspace audit. Confirm a task destination before adding a
76: section; a new task root still needs its normal initialization approval.
77: 
78: Maintain confirmed locations and affected navigation; preserve customized
79: behavioral text, stricter local gates, outside content, and block position. Do not
80: copy skill procedures into guidance, edit another owner's section, or regenerate
81: the whole file. Replace obsolete default "load skill to continue reading" cues
82: only when they conflict with read-only task discovery. If a local approval gate
83: conflicts with the default maintenance authorization, preserve the gate and
84: report the remaining restriction rather than silently overriding it. A source or
85: installed skill update, read-only retrieval, or ordinary conversation does not
86: itself authorize workspace changes.
87: 
88: Reread immediately before writing. Refuse duplicate, incomplete, nested, or
89: ambiguous markers and unsafe paths; reconcile concurrent edits without clobbering
90: human work. Read back, check the owned diff and links, and report partial failure
91: without discarding valid task work. An existing central workspace block requires
92: identified scope and migration approval; do not leave competing location tables.

---
name: fastapi
description: FastAPI best practices and conventions. Use when working with FastAPI APIs, Pydantic models, dependencies, streaming responses including Server-Sent Events (SSE), and serving frontend apps. Keeps FastAPI code clean and up to date with the latest features and patterns.
---

# FastAPI

Official FastAPI skill to write code with best practices, keeping up to date with new versions and features.

## Quick Reference

* Deployment: FastAPI Cloud is the official managed deployment option. See [the FastAPI Cloud reference](references/fastapi-cloud.md).
* Serve frontend apps: use `app.frontend()` or `router.frontend()` for built frontend assets; see [Serve Frontend Apps](#serve-frontend-apps).
* Frontend and API clients: reuse the project's generated SDK and generation setup. When adding a client, prefer generating it from the app's OpenAPI schema. Regenerate it when the API schema changes, and avoid editing generated files manually. See [Generating SDKs](https://fastapi.tiangolo.com/advanced/generate-clients/).
* Server-Sent Events (SSE): use `response_class=EventSourceResponse` and `yield`; see [Streaming](#streaming-json-lines-sse-bytes) and [the streaming reference](references/streaming.md).
* JSON Lines and byte streaming: see [the streaming reference](references/streaming.md).
* OpenTelemetry: use FastAPI's native traces, metrics, and logs. See [OpenTelemetry](#opentelemetry).
* Dependencies: use `Annotated[..., Depends(...)]`; see [Dependency Injection](#dependency-injection) and [the dependency injection reference](references/dependencies.md) for `yield`, scopes, concurrency limits, and class dependencies.
* Response models: prefer return types; use `response_model` when the public response schema differs from the internal return value; see [the response reference](references/responses.md).
* Request body limits: use `max_body_size` on the app, router, or path operation. See [Request Body Limits](#request-body-limits).
* Parameter models: `Query`, `Header`, `Cookie`, and `Form` support multiple models and optional groups. See [the parameter models reference](references/parameter-models.md) for declarations, form unions, and extra-field behavior.
* Pydantic models: do not use ellipsis or `RootModel`; see [the Pydantic reference](references/pydantic.md).
* Routing: declare router-level prefix, tags, and shared dependencies on the `APIRouter`; see [the path operation reference](references/path-operations.md).
* Tooling and related libraries: use uv, Ruff, ty, Asyncer, SQLModel, and HTTPX when applicable; see [the other tools reference](references/other-tools.md).

## Use the `fastapi` CLI

Run the development server on localhost with reload:

```bash
fastapi dev
```

Run the production server:

```bash
fastapi run
```

Prefer declaring the entrypoint in `pyproject.toml`:

```toml
[tool.fastapi]
entrypoint = "my_app.main:app"
```

When adding the entrypoint is not possible, or the user explicitly asks not to, pass the app file path:

```bash
fastapi dev my_app/main.py
```

## Deploy to FastAPI Cloud

FastAPI Cloud is the official managed deployment platform from the FastAPI team.

Deploy with `uv run fastapi deploy`.

For Cloud deployment and management tasks, read [the FastAPI Cloud reference](references/fastapi-cloud.md) and use an available FastAPI Cloud skill.

## Use `Annotated`

Always prefer the `Annotated` style for parameter and dependency declarations. It keeps function signatures working in other contexts, respects the types, and allows reusability.

Use `Annotated` for parameter declarations, including `Path`, `Query`, `Header`, etc.:

```python
from typing import Annotated

from fastapi import FastAPI, Path, Query

app = FastAPI()


@app.get("/items/{item_id}")
async def read_item(
    item_id: Annotated[int, Path(ge=1, description="The item ID")],
    q: Annotated[str | None, Query(max_length=50)] = None,
):
    return {"message": "Hello World"}
```

Use `Annotated` for dependencies with `Depends()`. Unless asked not to, create a new type alias for the dependency to allow reusing it:

```python
from typing import Annotated

from fastapi import Depends, FastAPI

app = FastAPI()


def get_current_user():
    return {"username": "johndoe"}


CurrentUserDep = Annotated[dict, Depends(get_current_user)]


@app.get("/items/")
async def read_item(current_user: CurrentUserDep):
    return {"message": "Hello World"}
```

## Do not use Ellipsis for *path operations* or Pydantic models

Do not use `...` as a default value for required parameters or model fields. It's not needed and not recommended.

```python
from typing import Annotated

from fastapi import FastAPI, Query
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field

app = FastAPI()


class Item(BaseModel):
    name: str
    description: str | None = None
    price: float = Field(gt=0)


@app.post("/items/")
async def create_item(item: Item, project_id: Annotated[int, Query()]):
    return item
```

See [the Pydantic reference](references/pydantic.md) for more details.

## Return Type or Response Model

When possible, include a return type. It will be used to validate, filter, document, and serialize the response.

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI
from pydantic import BaseModel

app = FastAPI()


class Item(BaseModel):
    name: str
    description: str | None = None


@app.get("/items/me")
async def get_item() -> Item:
    return Item(name="Plumbus", description="All-purpose home device")
```

Return types or response models filter data to avoid exposing sensitive information, and they let Pydantic serialize the data on the Rust side for performance.

Use `response_model` when the type you return is not the same as the public schema you want to validate, filter, document, and serialize. See [the response reference](references/responses.md).

## Performance

Do not use `ORJSONResponse` or `UJSONResponse`, they are deprecated.

Instead, declare a return type or response model. Pydantic will handle the data serialization on the Rust side.

## Including Routers

When declaring routers, prefer to add router-level parameters like prefix, tags, and shared dependencies to the router itself instead of in `include_router()`.

```python
from fastapi import APIRouter, Depends, FastAPI

app = FastAPI()


def get_current_user():
    return {"username": "johndoe"}


router = APIRouter(
    prefix="/items",
    tags=["items"],
    dependencies=[Depends(get_current_user)],
)


@router.get("/")
async def list_items():
    return []


app.include_router(router)
```

See [the path operation reference](references/path-operations.md) for more routing patterns.

## Request Body Limits

Use `max_body_size` when an application needs a limit on total HTTP request body bytes. The limit includes uploaded files and multipart overhead. Enforcement counts received bytes, so a Content-Length-only dependency is not a substitute.

Configure a default with `FastAPI(max_body_size=...)`, a router default with `APIRouter(max_body_size=...)`, or a specific limit on a path operation. More specific configured limits can raise or lower the default. `None` inherits, and `0` allows only an empty body. Without a configured limit, bodies remain unlimited.

Middleware that reads the body before routing uses the application limit, even when an upload endpoint allows more. This total limit is separate from per-file validation, parser field counts, and the spool threshold.

Use `form_limits` with `max_fields`, `max_files`, or `max_part_size` to configure automatic form parsing. Each key inherits independently from the router or app. `max_part_size` applies to text fields. Use `max_body_size` for a total byte limit that includes files.

See [Request Body Limits](https://fastapi.tiangolo.com/advanced/request-body-limits/) for an example.

## Serve Frontend Apps

Use `app.frontend()` to serve a built static frontend app, for example a directory generated by Vite, Astro, Angular, Svelte, Vue, or a similar tool.

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI

app = FastAPI()

app.frontend("/", directory="dist")
```

Use `router.frontend()` when the frontend belongs to an `APIRouter`; normal router prefix behavior applies when the router is included.

```python
from fastapi import APIRouter, FastAPI

app = FastAPI()
router = APIRouter(prefix="/admin")

router.frontend("/", directory="admin-dist")
app.include_router(router)
```

`app.frontend()` and `router.frontend()` are low-priority routes: regular API routes are matched first, then frontend files and client-side routing fallbacks. Use this for single-page apps and built frontend assets instead of mounting `StaticFiles` manually.

## OpenTelemetry

Prefer FastAPI's native OpenTelemetry support for request traces, metrics, and logs.

Install `fastapi[standard]` to include the SDK and HTTP/protobuf exporters. Enable automatic exporter setup with `FASTAPI_OTEL_AUTO_CONFIGURE=true`, set `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` to identify the app, and set `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` to the collector's HTTP/protobuf base URL. Use `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` when authentication is required. FastAPI uses providers configured by another telemetry library without needing automatic setup.

Use `FastAPI(telemetry={...})` for custom configuration, such as choosing signals or supplying providers.

See the [OpenTelemetry tutorial](https://fastapi.tiangolo.com/advanced/opentelemetry/) for configuration details.

## Dependency Injection

Use dependencies when the logic can't be declared in Pydantic validation, depends on external resources, needs cleanup with `yield`, or is shared across endpoints.

Apply shared dependencies at the router level via `dependencies=[Depends(...)]`.

When concurrent dependency calls could exhaust a shared resource, use `Depends(max_concurrency=...)`. Read [dependency concurrency](references/dependencies.md#limit-dependency-concurrency) to choose the limit and share it across routes.

See [the dependency injection reference](references/dependencies.md) for `yield` with `scope`, concurrency limits, and class dependencies.

## Async vs Sync *path operations*

Use `async` *path operations* only when fully certain that the logic called inside is compatible with async and await, and that it doesn't block.

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI

app = FastAPI()


@app.get("/async-items/")
async def read_async_items():
    data = await some_async_library.fetch_items()
    return data


@app.get("/items/")
def read_items():
    data = some_blocking_library.fetch_items()
    return data
```

In case of doubt, or by default, use regular `def` functions. They will be run in a threadpool so they don't block the event loop. The same rules apply to dependencies.

Make sure blocking code is not run inside of `async` functions. The logic will work, but will damage performance heavily.

When needing to mix blocking and async code, see Asyncer in [the other tools reference](references/other-tools.md).

## Streaming (JSON Lines, SSE, bytes)

To stream Server-Sent Events, use `response_class=EventSourceResponse` and `yield` items from the endpoint.

```python
from collections.abc import AsyncIterable

from fastapi import FastAPI
from fastapi.sse import EventSourceResponse, ServerSentEvent

app = FastAPI()


@app.get("/events", response_class=EventSourceResponse)
async def stream_events() -> AsyncIterable[ServerSentEvent]:
    yield ServerSentEvent(data={"status": "started"}, event="status", id="1")
```

Plain objects are automatically JSON-serialized as `data:` fields. Use `ServerSentEvent` for full control over SSE fields (`event`, `id`, `retry`, `comment`) and `raw_data` for pre-formatted strings.

See [the streaming reference](references/streaming.md) for JSON Lines, Server-Sent Events (`EventSourceResponse`, `ServerSentEvent`), and byte streaming (`StreamingResponse`) patterns.

## Tooling

See [the other tools reference](references/other-tools.md) for details on uv, Ruff, ty for package management, linting, type checking, formatting, etc.

## Other Libraries

See [the other tools reference](references/other-tools.md) for details on other libraries:

* Asyncer for handling async and await, concurrency, mixing async and blocking code, prefer it over AnyIO or asyncio.
* SQLModel for working with SQL databases, prefer it over SQLAlchemy.
* HTTPX for interacting with HTTP (other APIs), prefer it over Requests.

## Do not use Pydantic RootModels

Do not use Pydantic `RootModel`; instead use regular type annotations with `Annotated` and Pydantic validation utilities.

```python
from typing import Annotated

from fastapi import Body, FastAPI
from pydantic import Field

app = FastAPI()


@app.post("/items/")
async def create_items(items: Annotated[list[int], Field(min_length=1), Body()]):
    return items
```

FastAPI supports these type annotations and will create a Pydantic `TypeAdapter` for them, so types work normally without custom wrapper models. See [the Pydantic reference](references/pydantic.md).

## Use one HTTP operation per function

Don't mix HTTP operations in a single function. Having one function per HTTP operation helps separate concerns and organize the code.

```python
from fastapi import FastAPI
from pydantic import BaseModel

app = FastAPI()


class Item(BaseModel):
    name: str


@app.get("/items/")
async def list_items():
    return []


@app.post("/items/")
async def create_item(item: Item):
    return item
```

See [the path operation reference](references/path-operations.md) for more examples.

# [Using uv in Docker](#using-uv-in-docker)

## [Getting started](#getting-started)

Tip

Check out the [`uv-docker-example`](https://github.com/astral-sh/uv-docker-example) project for an example of best practices when using uv to build an application in Docker.

uv provides both *distroless* Docker images, which are useful for [copying uv binaries](#installing-uv) into your own image builds, and images derived from popular base images, which are useful for using uv in a container. The distroless images do not contain anything but the uv binaries. In contrast, the derived images include an operating system with uv pre-installed.

As an example, to run uv in a container using a Debian-based image:

```
$ docker run --rm -it ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:debian uv --help
```

### [Available images](#available-images)

The following distroless images are available:

- `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest`

- `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:{major}.{minor}.{patch}`, e.g., `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.13.9`

- `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:{major}.{minor}`, e.g., `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.13` (the latest patch version)

And the following derived images are available:

- Based on `alpine:3.23`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:alpine`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:alpine3.23`

- Based on `alpine:3.22`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:alpine3.22`

- Based on `debian:trixie-slim`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:debian-slim`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:trixie-slim`

- Based on `buildpack-deps:trixie`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:debian`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:trixie`

- Based on `dhi.io/alpine-base:3.23`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:alpine-dhi`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:alpine3.23-dhi`

- Based on `dhi.io/debian-base:trixie-debian13`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:debian-dhi`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:trixie-dhi`

- Based on `dhi/python:3.x`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.14-dhi`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.13-dhi`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.12-dhi`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.11-dhi`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.10-dhi`

- Based on `python3.x-alpine`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.15-alpine`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.15-alpine3.23`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.14-alpine`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.14-alpine3.23`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.13-alpine`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.13-alpine3.23`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.12-alpine`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.12-alpine3.23`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.11-alpine`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.11-alpine3.23`

- Based on `python3.x-trixie`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.15-trixie`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.14-trixie`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.13-trixie`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.12-trixie`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.11-trixie`

- Based on `python3.x-slim-trixie`:

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.15-trixie-slim`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.14-trixie-slim`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.13-trixie-slim`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.12-trixie-slim`

  - `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:python3.11-trixie-slim`

As with the distroless image, each derived image is published with uv version tags as `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:{major}.{minor}.{patch}-{base}` and `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:{major}.{minor}-{base}`, e.g., `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.13.9-alpine`.

In addition, starting with `0.8` each derived image also sets `UV_TOOL_BIN_DIR` to `/usr/local/bin` to allow `uv tool install` to work as expected with the default user.

For more details, see the [GitHub Container](https://github.com/astral-sh/uv/pkgs/container/uv) page.

### [Installing uv](#installing-uv)

Use one of the above images with uv pre-installed or install uv by copying the binary from the official distroless Docker image:

Dockerfile

```
FROM python:3.12-slim-trixie
COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest /uv /uvx /bin/
```

Or, with the installer:

Dockerfile

~~~
FROM python:3.12-slim-trixie

# The installer requires curl (and certificates) to download the release archive
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends curl ca-certificates

# Download the latest installer
ADD https://astral.sh/uv/install.sh /uv-installer.sh

# Run the installer then remove it
RUN sh /uv-installer.sh && rm /uv-installer.sh

# Ensure the installed binary is on the `PATH`
ENV PATH="/root/.local/bin/:$PATH"
~~~

Note this requires `curl` to be available.

In either case, it is best practice to pin to a specific uv version, e.g., with:

```
COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.13.9 /uv /uvx /bin/
```

Tip

While the Dockerfile example above pins to a specific tag, it's also possible to pin a specific SHA256. Pinning a specific SHA256 is considered best practice in environments that require reproducible builds as tags can be moved across different commit SHAs.

```
COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv@sha256:32a7bb39b496babbd997b0a39a4d66c126bdaf5661b302bf6f06abda8deca6ff /uv /uvx /bin/
```

Or, with the installer:

```
ADD https://astral.sh/uv/0.13.9/install.sh /uv-installer.sh
```

### [Installing a project](#installing-a-project)

If you're using uv to manage your project, you can copy it into the image and install it:

Dockerfile

```
# Copy the project into the image
COPY . /app

# Disable development dependencies
ENV UV_NO_DEV=1

# Sync the project into a new environment, asserting the lockfile is up to date
WORKDIR /app
RUN uv sync --locked
```

Important

It is best practice to add `.venv` to a [`.dockerignore` file](https://docs.docker.com/build/concepts/context/#dockerignore-files) in your repository to prevent it from being included in image builds. The project virtual environment is dependent on your local platform and should be created from scratch in the image.

Then, to start your application by default:

Dockerfile

~~~
# Presuming there is a `my_app` command provided by the project
CMD ["uv", "run", "my_app"]
~~~

Tip

It is best practice to use [intermediate layers](#intermediate-layers) separating installation of dependencies and the project itself to improve Docker image build times.

See a complete example in the [`uv-docker-example` project](https://github.com/astral-sh/uv-docker-example/blob/main/Dockerfile).

### [Using the environment](#using-the-environment)

Once the project is installed, you can either *activate* the project virtual environment by placing its binary directory at the front of the path:

Dockerfile

```
ENV PATH="/app/.venv/bin:$PATH"
```

Or, you can use `uv run` for any commands that require the environment:

Dockerfile

```
RUN uv run some_script.py
```

Tip

Alternatively, the [`UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT` setting](../../../concepts/projects/config/#project-environment-path) can be set before syncing to install to the system Python environment and skip environment activation entirely.

### [Using installed tools](#using-installed-tools)

To use installed tools, ensure the [tool bin directory](../../../concepts/tools/#tool-executables) is on the path:

Dockerfile

```
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
RUN uv tool install cowsay
```

```
$ docker run -it $(docker build -q .) /bin/bash -c "cowsay -t hello"
  _____
| hello |
  =====
     \
      \
        ^__^
        (oo)\_______
        (__)\       )\/\
            ||----w |
            ||     ||
```

Note

The tool bin directory's location can be determined by running the `uv tool dir --bin` command in the container.

Alternatively, it can be set to a constant location:

Dockerfile

```
ENV UV_TOOL_BIN_DIR=/opt/uv-bin/
```

## [Developing in a container](#developing-in-a-container)

When developing, it's useful to mount the project directory into a container. With this setup, changes to the project can be immediately reflected in a containerized service without rebuilding the image. However, it is important *not* to include the project virtual environment (`.venv`) in the mount, because the virtual environment is platform specific and the one built for the image should be kept.

### [Mounting the project with `docker run`](#mounting-the-project-with-docker-run)

Bind mount the project (in the working directory) to `/app` while retaining the `.venv` directory with an [anonymous volume](https://docs.docker.com/engine/storage/#volumes):

```
$ docker run --rm --volume .:/app --volume /app/.venv [...]
```

Tip

The `--rm` flag is included to ensure the container and anonymous volume are cleaned up when the container exits.

See a complete example in the [`uv-docker-example` project](https://github.com/astral-sh/uv-docker-example/blob/main/run.sh).

### [Configuring `watch` with `docker compose`](#configuring-watch-with-docker-compose)

When using Docker compose, more sophisticated tooling is available for container development. The [`watch`](https://docs.docker.com/compose/file-watch/#compose-watch-versus-bind-mounts) option allows for greater granularity than is practical with a bind mount and supports triggering updates to the containerized service when files change.

Note

This feature requires Compose 2\.22.0 which is bundled with Docker Desktop 4\.24.

Configure `watch` in your [Docker compose file](https://docs.docker.com/compose/compose-application-model/#the-compose-file) to mount the project directory without syncing the project virtual environment and to rebuild the image when the configuration changes:

compose.yaml

~~~
services:
  example:
    build: .

    # ...

    develop:
      # Create a `watch` configuration to update the app
      #
      watch:
        # Sync the working directory with the `/app` directory in the container
        - action: sync
          path: .
          target: /app
          # Exclude the project virtual environment
          ignore:
            - .venv/

        # Rebuild the image on changes to the `pyproject.toml`
        - action: rebuild
          path: ./pyproject.toml
~~~

Then, run `docker compose watch` to run the container with the development setup.

See a complete example in the [`uv-docker-example` project](https://github.com/astral-sh/uv-docker-example/blob/main/compose.yml).

## [Optimizations](#optimizations)

### [Compiling bytecode](#compiling-bytecode)

Compiling Python source files to bytecode is typically desirable for production images as it tends to improve startup time (at the cost of increased installation time and image size).

To enable bytecode compilation, use the `--compile-bytecode` flag:

Dockerfile

```
RUN uv python install --compile-bytecode
RUN uv sync --compile-bytecode
```

Alternatively, you can set the `UV_COMPILE_BYTECODE` environment variable to ensure that all commands within the Dockerfile compile bytecode:

Dockerfile

```
ENV UV_COMPILE_BYTECODE=1
```

Note

uv will only compile the standard library of *managed* Python versions during `uv python install`. The distributor of unmanaged Python versions decides if the standard library is pre-compiled. For example, the official `python` image will not have a compiled standard library.

### [Caching](#caching)

A [cache mount](https://docs.docker.com/build/guide/mounts/#add-a-cache-mount) can be used to improve performance across builds:

Dockerfile

```
ENV UV_LINK_MODE=copy

RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    uv sync
```

Changing the [`UV_LINK_MODE`](../../../reference/settings/#link-mode) silences warnings about not being able to link files since the cache and sync target are on separate file systems.

If you're not mounting the cache, image size can be reduced by using the `--no-cache` flag or setting `UV_NO_CACHE`.

By default, managed Python installations are not cached before being installed. Setting `UV_PYTHON_CACHE_DIR` can be used in combination with a cache mount:

Dockerfile

```
ENV UV_PYTHON_CACHE_DIR=/root/.cache/uv/python

RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    uv python install
```

Note

The cache directory's location can be determined by running the `uv cache dir` command in the container.

Alternatively, the cache can be set to a constant location:

Dockerfile

```
ENV UV_CACHE_DIR=/opt/uv-cache/
```

### [Intermediate layers](#intermediate-layers)

If you're using uv to manage your project, you can improve build times by moving your transitive dependency installation into its own layer via the `--no-install` options.

`uv sync --no-install-project` will install the dependencies of the project but not the project itself. Since the project changes frequently, but its dependencies are generally static, this can be a big time saver.

Dockerfile

~~~
# Install uv
FROM python:3.12-slim
COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest /uv /uvx /bin/

# Change the working directory to the `app` directory
WORKDIR /app

# Install dependencies
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    --mount=type=bind,source=uv.lock,target=uv.lock \
    uv sync --frozen --no-install-project

# Copy the project into the image
COPY . /app

# Sync the project
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    uv sync --locked
~~~

Note that only the `uv.lock` file is mounted for the first sync. The `pyproject.toml` and project *contents* are copied into the image before the final `uv sync` command.

Tip

If you want to remove additional, specific packages from the sync, use `--no-install-package <name>`.

#### [Intermediate layers in workspaces](#intermediate-layers-in-workspaces)

If you're using a [workspace](../../../concepts/projects/workspaces/), use the `--no-install-workspace` flag to exclude the project *and* any workspace members from the initial sync:

Dockerfile

```
# Install uv
FROM python:3.12-slim
COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest /uv /uvx /bin/

WORKDIR /app

RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    --mount=type=bind,source=uv.lock,target=uv.lock \
    uv sync --frozen --no-install-workspace

COPY . /app

RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    uv sync --locked
```

uv cannot assert that the `uv.lock` file is up-to-date without the project and workspace member `pyproject.toml` files, so we use `--frozen` instead of `--locked` to skip the check during the initial sync. The next sync, after all the workspace members have been copied, can still use `--locked` and will validate that the lockfile is correct for all workspace members.

### [Non-editable installs](#non-editable-installs)

By default, uv installs projects and workspace members in editable mode, such that changes to the source code are immediately reflected in the environment.

`uv sync` and `uv run` both accept a `--no-editable` flag, which instructs uv to install the project in non-editable mode, removing any dependency on the source code.

In the context of a multi-stage Docker image, `--no-editable` can be used to include the project in the synced virtual environment from one stage, then copy the virtual environment alone (and not the source code) into the final image.

For example:

Dockerfile

~~~
# Install uv
FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder
COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest /uv /uvx /bin/

# Use the system Python across both stages
ENV UV_PYTHON_DOWNLOADS=0

# Change the working directory to the `app` directory
WORKDIR /app

# Install dependencies
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    --mount=type=bind,source=uv.lock,target=uv.lock \
    uv sync --frozen --no-install-project --no-editable

# Copy the project into the intermediate image
COPY . /app

# Sync the project
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/uv \
    uv sync --locked --no-editable

FROM python:3.12-slim

# Copy the environment, but not the source code
COPY --from=builder /app/.venv /app/.venv

# Run the application
CMD ["/app/.venv/bin/hello"]
~~~

### [Using uv temporarily](#using-uv-temporarily)

If uv isn't needed in the final image, the binary can be mounted in each invocation:

Dockerfile

```
RUN --mount=from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv,source=/uv,target=/bin/uv \
    uv sync
```

## [Using the pip interface](#using-the-pip-interface)

### [Installing a package](#installing-a-package)

The system Python environment is safe to use in this context, since a container is already isolated. The `--system` flag can be used to install in the system environment:

Dockerfile

```
RUN uv pip install --system ruff
```

To use the system Python environment by default, set the `UV_SYSTEM_PYTHON` variable:

Dockerfile

```
ENV UV_SYSTEM_PYTHON=1
```

Alternatively, a virtual environment can be created and activated:

Dockerfile

```
RUN uv venv /opt/venv
# Use the virtual environment automatically
ENV VIRTUAL_ENV=/opt/venv
# Place entry points in the environment at the front of the path
ENV PATH="/opt/venv/bin:$PATH"
```

When using a virtual environment, the `--system` flag should be omitted from uv invocations:

Dockerfile

```
RUN uv pip install ruff
```

### [Installing requirements](#installing-requirements)

To install requirements files, copy them into the container:

Dockerfile

```
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN uv pip install -r requirements.txt
```

### [Installing a project](#installing-a-project_1)

When installing a project alongside requirements, it is best practice to separate copying the requirements from the rest of the source code. This allows the dependencies of the project (which do not change often) to be cached separately from the project itself (which changes very frequently).

Dockerfile

```
COPY pyproject.toml .
RUN uv pip install -r pyproject.toml
COPY . .
RUN uv pip install -e .
```

## [Verifying image provenance](#verifying-image-provenance)

The Docker images are signed during the build process to provide proof of their origin. These attestations can be used to verify that an image was produced from an official channel.

For example, you can verify the attestations with the [GitHub CLI tool `gh`](https://cli.github.com/):

```
$ gh attestation verify --owner astral-sh oci://ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest
Loaded digest sha256:xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx for oci://ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest
Loaded 1 attestation from GitHub API

The following policy criteria will be enforced:
- OIDC Issuer must match:................... https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com
- Source Repository Owner URI must match:... https://github.com/astral-sh
- Predicate type must match:................ https://slsa.dev/provenance/v1
- Subject Alternative Name must match regex: (?i)^https://github.com/astral-sh/

✓ Verification succeeded!

sha256:xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx was attested by:
REPO          PREDICATE_TYPE                  WORKFLOW
astral-sh/uv  https://slsa.dev/provenance/v1  .github/workflows/build-docker.yml@refs/heads/main
```

This tells you that the specific Docker image was built by the official uv GitHub release workflow and hasn't been tampered with since.

GitHub attestations build on the [sigstore.dev infrastructure](https://www.sigstore.dev/). As such you can also use the [`cosign` command](https://github.com/sigstore/cosign) to verify the attestation blob against the (multi-platform) manifest for `uv`:

```
$ REPO=astral-sh/uv
$ gh attestation download --repo $REPO oci://ghcr.io/${REPO}:latest
Wrote attestations to file sha256:xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx.jsonl.
Any previous content has been overwritten

The trusted metadata is now available at sha256:xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx.jsonl
$ docker buildx imagetools inspect ghcr.io/${REPO}:latest --format "{{json .Manifest}}" > manifest.json
$ cosign verify-blob-attestation \
    --new-bundle-format \
    --bundle "$(jq -r .digest manifest.json).jsonl"  \
    --certificate-oidc-issuer="https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com" \
    --certificate-identity-regexp="^https://github\.com/${REPO}/.*" \
    <(jq -j '.|del(.digest,.size)' manifest.json)
Verified OK
```

Tip

These examples use `latest`, but best practice is to verify the attestation for a specific version tag, e.g., `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.13.9`, or (even better) the specific image digest, such as `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv@sha256:32a7bb39b496babbd997b0a39a4d66c126bdaf5661b302bf6f06abda8deca6ff`.

10: **Goal:** Build the local persistent trip/day foundation consumed by the real ChatGPT MCP Apps experiment, with a small mobile-usable web interface and shared backend operations.

22: ## Deliverables and success bar
23: 
24: Use one synthetic trip, one day and a few activities: a fixed booked anchor, a selected flexible stop and an optional alternative. Include synthetic evidence metadata marked honestly as unverified; do not read/import private proofs to make fixtures.
25: 
26: 1. A phone-sized web view displays the persisted day with stable activity IDs, order, meaningful status distinctions and formatted activity notes.
27: 2. Familiar Title/Notes editing supports ordinary paragraphs, bullets, bold and links without requiring raw Markdown. Saving and reopening preserve the supported formatting; unsafe HTML and links cannot execute.
28: 3. A manual reorder, traveler-reported visited state and notes edit save through FastAPI domain operations and survive reload and normal application/database-container restart with the volume retained. Document that a volume is not a backup and deleting it loses local data.
29: 4. The domain contract supports reading the day, proposing a bounded change, inspecting/accepting that proposal and recording the actor, revision and result. The accepted plan does not change merely because a proposal was created. The later MCP adapter must reuse these operations.
30: 5. A stale edit or approval cannot silently overwrite a newer revision; repeated acceptance cannot apply twice. Return useful saved/conflict outcomes and record what changed without building a general history UI.
31: 6. Moving or marking an activity visited does not mutate or verify its linked supplier booking. Attaching synthetic evidence is not verification. Optional alternatives do not become selected commitments automatically.
32: 7. Supply focused tests, PostgreSQL migrations/seed data, a reproducible Docker/uv local run path, and the actual OpenAPI/domain contract plus verification notes for the integration task.

37: - Preserve explicit timezone meaning and approximate/unset activity timing without building a generic temporal system. Use PostgreSQL-compatible migrations rather than changing the primary store to Markdown, SQLite or a time-series database.
38: - The local slice may use a tightly scoped local-only development actor, clearly labeled as not production authentication. Expose its access contract/gaps to the integration task; do not assume that actor may be forwarded through a tunnel.
39: - Do not require full event sourcing, generalized undo, preference management, multi-user invitations, live co-editing, real files or external booking operations. Preserve approval/revision semantics without extending the experiment into the full MVP.

---
title: "Basic Usage"
description: "Learn the fundamental operations for working with Markdown in Tiptap. This guide covers parsing, serialization, and common tasks."
canonical_url: "https://tiptap.dev/docs/editor/markdown/getting-started/basic-usage"
---

# Basic Usage

Learn the fundamental operations for working with Markdown in Tiptap. This guide covers parsing, serialization, and common tasks.

This guide covers the core operations for working with Markdown: parsing Markdown into your editor and serializing editor content back to Markdown.

## Getting Markdown from the Editor

Use `getMarkdown()` to serialize your editor content to Markdown:

```typescript
const markdown = editor.getMarkdown()
console.log(markdown)
// # Hello
//
// This is a **test**.
```

> **Interactive demo:** [Serialize](https://embed.tiptap.dev/preview/Markdown/Serialize)

## Setting Content from Markdown

All content commands support the `contentType` option:

```typescript
// 1. Initial content
const editor = new Editor({
  extensions: [StarterKit, Markdown],
  content: '# Hello World\n\nThis is **markdown**!',
  contentType: 'markdown',
})

// 2. Replace all content
editor.commands.setContent('# New Content', { contentType: 'markdown' })

// 3. Insert at cursor
editor.commands.insertContent('**Bold** text', { contentType: 'markdown' })

// 4. Insert at specific position
editor.commands.insertContentAt(10, '## Heading', { contentType: 'markdown' })

// 5. Replace a range
editor.commands.insertContentAt({ from: 10, to: 20 }, '**Replace**', { contentType: 'markdown' })
```

> **Interactive demo:** [Parse](https://embed.tiptap.dev/preview/Markdown/Parse)

## Using the MarkdownManager Directly

For more control, access the `MarkdownManager` via `editor.markdown`:

```typescript
// Parse Markdown to JSON
const json = editor.markdown.parse('# Hello World')
console.log(json)
// { type: 'doc', content: [...] }

// Serialize JSON to Markdown
const markdown = editor.markdown.serialize(json)
console.log(markdown)
// # Hello World
```

This is useful when working with JSON content outside the editor context.

## GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM)

Enable GFM for features like tables and task lists:

```typescript
import { Markdown } from '@tiptap/markdown'
import StarterKit from '@tiptap/starter-kit'
import Table from '@tiptap/extension-table'
import TableRow from '@tiptap/extension-table-row'
import TableCell from '@tiptap/extension-table-cell'
import TableHeader from '@tiptap/extension-table-header'
import TaskList from '@tiptap/extension-task-list'
import TaskItem from '@tiptap/extension-task-item'

const editor = new Editor({
  extensions: [
    StarterKit,
    Table,
    TableRow,
    TableCell,
    TableHeader,
    TaskList,
    TaskItem,
    Markdown.configure({
      markedOptions: { gfm: true },
    }),
  ],
})
```

## Inline Formatting

Standard Markdown formatting works automatically:

```typescript
const markdown = `
**bold text** or __bold text__
*italic text* or _italic text_
***bold and italic***
[Link Text](https://example.com)
\`inline code\`
`

editor.commands.setContent(markdown, { contentType: 'markdown' })
const result = editor.getMarkdown() // Formatting preserved
```

## Working with Block Elements

Block elements like headings, lists, and code blocks work as expected:

### Headings

```typescript
const markdown = `
# Heading 1
## Heading 2
### Heading 3
#### Heading 4
##### Heading 5
###### Heading 6
`
```

### Lists

```typescript
// Unordered lists
const markdown = `
- Item 1
- Item 2
  - Nested item 2.1
  - Nested item 2.2
- Item 3
`

// Ordered lists
const markdown = `
1. First item
2. Second item
   1. Nested item 2.1
   2. Nested item 2.2
3. Third item
`
```

### Code Blocks

```typescript
const markdown = `
\`\`\`javascript
function hello() {
  console.log('Hello World')
}
\`\`\`
`
```

### Blockquotes

```typescript
const markdown = `
> This is a blockquote.
> It can span multiple lines.
>
> > And can be nested.
`
```

## Handling HTML in Markdown

The Markdown extension can parse HTML embedded in Markdown using Tiptap's existing `parseHTML` methods:

```typescript
const markdown = `
# Heading

<div class="custom">
  <p>This HTML will be parsed</p>
</div>

Regular **Markdown** continues here.
`

editor.commands.setContent(markdown, { contentType: 'markdown' })
```

The HTML is parsed according to your extensions' `parseHTML` rules, allowing you to support custom HTML nodes.

## Best Practices

**Always use `contentType`** and set it to `markdown` when setting Markdown content (otherwise it's treated as HTML):

```typescript
editor.commands.setContent(markdown, { contentType: 'markdown' })
```

**Include all needed extensions** or content may be lost:

```typescript
const editor = new Editor({
  extensions: [StarterKit, Markdown], // StarterKit covers most common nodes
})
```

**Test round-trip conversion** to ensure your custom Markdown content survives parse → serialize:

```typescript
editor.commands.setContent('# Hello **World**', { contentType: 'markdown' })
const result = editor.getMarkdown() // Should match original
```

## Key Components

### `MarkdownManager`

The `MarkdownManager` class is the core engine that handles parsing and serialization. It:

- Wraps and configures the MarkedJS instance
- Maintains a registry of extension handlers
- Creates the [Lexer](../glossary/#lexer) instance and registers all [Tokenizers](../glossary/#tokenizer).
- Coordinates between Markdown tokens and Tiptap JSON nodes

### `Markdown` extension

The `Markdown` extension is the main extension that you add to your editor. It provides:

- Overrides for all **content-related** commands on the editor to support Markdown input/output
- The `getMarkdown()` method to serialize content as Markdown
- The `setContent()` command with `contentType: 'markdown'` option to parse Markdown input
- Access to the [`MarkdownManager`](#using-the-markdownmanager-directly) instance via `editor.markdown`

### Extension Handlers

Each Tiptap extension can provide Markdown support by configuring the extension:

```typescript
const MyExtension = Node.create({
  // ...

  renderMarkdown: (token, helpers) => { /* ... */ },
  parseMarkdown: (node, helpers) => { /* ... */ },
  markdownTokenizer: { /* ... */ },
})
```

The handlers translate between Markdown tokens and Tiptap nodes in both directions and are automatically registered
by the [`MarkdownManager`](#using-the-markdownmanager-directly), creating [Tokenizers](../glossary/#tokenizer) out of them and registering those to the [Lexer](../glossary/#lexer).

Learn more about:

- [`renderMarkdown`](../advanced-usage/custom-serializing)
- [`parseMarkdown`](../advanced-usage/custom-parsing)
- [`markdownTokenizer`](../advanced-usage/custom-tokenizer)


---

## Assistant

Docker, Node and uv are available. I’ll use the personal Python environment you selected and keep the services bound to localhost.

For the first slice, I’ll use one revision number per day: proposals leave the plan unchanged until accepted, and stale edits return a conflict. The UI will clearly separate fixed bookings, flexible stops, optional ideas and traveler-reported visits.

---
title: Minimize Serialization at RSC Boundaries
impact: HIGH
impactDescription: reduces data transfer size
tags: server, rsc, serialization, props
---

## Minimize Serialization at RSC Boundaries

The React Server/Client boundary serializes all object properties into strings and embeds them in the HTML response and subsequent RSC requests. This serialized data directly impacts page weight and load time, so **size matters a lot**. Only pass fields that the client actually uses.

**Incorrect (serializes all 50 fields):**

```tsx
async function Page() {
  const user = await fetchUser()  // 50 fields
  return <Profile user={user} />
}

'use client'
function Profile({ user }: { user: User }) {
  return <div>{user.name}</div>  // uses 1 field
}
```

**Correct (serializes only 1 field):**

```tsx
async function Page() {
  const user = await fetchUser()
  return <Profile name={user.name} />
}

'use client'
function Profile({ name }: { name: string }) {
  return <div>{name}</div>
}
```


**Tool: webfetch**

**Input:**
```json
{
  "url": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vercel-labs/agent-skills/main/skills/react-best-practices/rules/async-parallel.md"
}
```

---
title: Promise.all() for Independent Operations
impact: CRITICAL
impactDescription: 2-10× improvement
tags: async, parallelization, promises, waterfalls
---

## Promise.all() for Independent Operations

When async operations have no interdependencies, execute them concurrently using `Promise.all()`.

**Incorrect (sequential execution, 3 round trips):**

```typescript
const user = await fetchUser()
const posts = await fetchPosts()
const comments = await fetchComments()
```

**Correct (parallel execution, 1 round trip):**

```typescript
const [user, posts, comments] = await Promise.all([
  fetchUser(),
  fetchPosts(),
  fetchComments()
])
```

     attributes: { role: \"textbox\", \"aria-multiline\": \"true\", \"aria-labelledby\": labelId },\n+    },\n+    onUpdate: ({ editor }) => onChange(editor.getMarkdown()),\n+  });\n+\n+  return <div onClick={() => editor?.chain().focus().toggleBulletList().run()}>\n+        Bullet list\n+      </button>\n+      <button type=\"button\" disabled={!editor} onClick={() => editor?.chain().focus().unsetLink().run()}>\n+        Remove link\n+      </button>\n+    </div>\n+    <EditorContent editor={editor} />\n+    <div className=\"link-controls\">\n+      <label>Link url : \"\";\n+      } catch { return \"\"; }\n+    }}\n+    components={{ a: ({ children, href }) => <a href={href} rel=\"noreferrer noopener\">{children}</a> }}\n+  >{markdown}</ReactMarkdown></div>;\n+}\n*** Add File: web/components/day-workspace.tsx\n+\"use client\";\n+\n+import { useCallback, useEffect, useState } from \"react\";\n+import { Activity, ApiError, Change, Day, Proposal, Saved, api } from \"@/lib/api\";\n+import { FormattedNotes } from \"./formatted-notes\";\n+import { NotesEditor } from \"./notes-editor\";\n+\n+function activityTime(activity: Activity) {\n+  if (!activity.local_start) return \"Any time\";\n+  return `${activity.timing === \"approximate\" ? An intervening change will be rejected, not overwritten.</p>\n+      <div className=\"actions\"><button className=\"primary\" type=\"submit\">Save activity</button>\n+        <button type=\"button\" onClick={cancel}>Cancel</button></div>\n+    </fieldset>\n+  </form>;\n+}\n+\n+function OrderPreview({ title, activities }: { title: string; activities: Activity[] }) {\n+  return <div><h4>{title}</h4><ol className=\"preview-order\">{activities.map(activity => <li key={activity.id}>\n+    <span>{activity.title}</span><small>{activityTime(activity)} · {activity.selection === \"optional\" ? <div className=\"preview-columns\">\n+      <OrderPreview title=\"Before\" activities={proposal.before_day.activities} />\n+      <OrderPreview title=\"Proposed order\" activities={proposal.preview_activities} />\n+    </div> : edit && before && after ? <div className=\"preview-columns\">\n+      <div><h4>Before: {before.title}</h4><FormattedNotes markdown={before.notes_markdown} /></div>\n+      <div><h4>Proposed: {after.title}</h4><FormattedNotes markdown={after.notes_markdown} /></div>\n+    </div> : <p>Traveler-reported visited state: {proposal.change.kind === \"set_visited\" && (proposal.change.visited ? \"visited\" : \"not visited\")}. This does not verify attendance or a booking.</p>}\n+    <p className=\"hint\">Activity order is not a new schedule. Fixed booking times and optional selections stay unchanged.</p>\n+    {stale ? <p className=\"conflict-note\">This proposal is stale. The day is now revision {revision}; create a fresh proposal after reviewing the current day.</p> :\n+      <button className=\"primary\" disabled={pending} onClick={accept}>Accept this proposal</button>}\n+  </article>;\n+}\n+\n+export function DayWorkspace({ dayId }: { dayId: string }) {\n+  const [day, setDay] = useState<Day | null>(null);\n+  const [proposals, setProposals] = useState<Proposal[]>([]);\n+  const [editing, setEditing] = useState<{ activity: Activity; revision: number } | null>(null);\n+  const [pending, setPending] = useState(false);\n+  const [loading, setLoading] = useState(true);\n+  const [feedback, setFeedback] = useState(\"\");\n+  const [error, setError] = useState(\"\");\n+  const [conflict, setConflict] = useState(false);\n+  const base = `days/${dayId}`;\n+\n+  const reload = useCallback(async () => {\n+    // Both operations are independent; neither result is a separate write authority.\n+    const [current, reviews] = await Promise.all([\n+      api<Day>(base), api<Proposal[]>(`${base}/proposals`),\n+    ]);\n+    setDay(current); setProposals(reviews);\n+  }, [base]);\n+\n+  useEffect(() => {\n+    let active = true;\n+    Promise.all([api<Day>(base), api<Proposal[]>(`${base}/proposals`)]).then(([current, reviews]) => {\n+      if (active) { setDay(current); setProposals(reviews); }\n+    }).catch(err => { if (active) setError(err.message); })\n+      .finally(() => { if (active) setLoading(false); });\n+    return () => { active = false; };\n+  }, [base]);\n+\n+  async function refresh() {\n+    setPending(true); setError(\"\");\n+    try { await reload(); setConflict(false); setFeedback(\"Loaded the current saved day. Any open edit is kept for your review.\"); }\n+    catch (err) { setError((err as Error).message); }\n+    finally { setPending(false); }\n+  }\n+\n+  async function save(change: Change, revision: number): Promise<boolean> {\n+    setPending(true); setError(\"\"); setFeedback(\"\");\n+    try {\n+      const result = await api<Saved>(`${base}/changes`, { expected_revision: revision, change });\n+      setDay(result.day); setConflict(false);\n+      setFeedback(result.outcome === \"unchanged\" ? \"No changes to save.\" : `Saved · revision ${result.day.revision}`);\n+      return true;\n+    } catch (err) {\n+      setError((err as Error).message);\n+      if (err instanceof ApiError && err.status === 409) setConflict(true);\n+      return false;\n+    } finally { setPending(false); }\n+  }\n+\n+  async function proposeSwap() {\n+    if (!day) return;\n+    const flexible = day.activities.filter(activity => activity.flexibility === \"flexible\");\n+    if (flexible.length < 2) return;\n+    const ids = day.activities.map(activity => activity.id);\n+    const first = ids.indexOf(flexible[0].id), second = ids.indexOf(flexible[1].id);\n+    [ids[first], ids[second]] = [ids[second], ids[first]];\n+    setPending(true); setError(\"\"); setFeedback(\"\");\n+    try {\n+      const proposal = await api<Proposal>(`${base}/proposals`, {\n+        expected_revision: day.revision,\n+        change: { kind: \"reorder\", activity_ids: ids },\n+        reason: \"Swap the two flexible stops for review, keeping the museum booking fixed and the garden optional.\",\n+      });\n+      setProposals(previous => [proposal, ...previous]);\n+      setFeedback(\"Proposal created. <p role=\"status\" className=\"loading\">Loading your saved day…</p> : <>\n+        {day && <section className=\"day-heading\">\n+          <p className=\"eyebrow\">{day.trip_title}</p>\n+          <h1>{day.title}</h1>\n+          <p className=\"day-meta\">{new Intl.DateTimeFormat(\"en-GB\", { weekday: \"long\", day: \"numeric\", month: \"long\", year: \"numeric\", timeZone: \"UTC\" }).format(new Date(`${day.date}T12:00:00Z`))} <span>· {day.timezone}</span></p>\n+          <p className=\"intro\">One fixed anchor. A little space for the rest.</p>\n+        </section>}\n+        <div className=\"save-bar\"><span>{day ? `Saved day · revision ${day.revision}` : \"The day could not be loaded\"}</span>\n+          <button disabled={pending} onClick={refresh}>{pending ? \"Working…\" : \"Refresh saved day\"}</button></div>\n+        <div className=\"feedback\" aria-live=\"polite\" role=\"status\">{feedback}</div>\n+        {error && <div className=\"error-panel\" role=\"alert\"><p>{error}</p>\n+          {conflict && <p>Nothing from this request was applied. Refresh the saved day; open edits stay intact so you can copy or compare them. ${activity.flexibility === \"fixed\" ? \"fixed\" : \"\"}`}>{activity.selection === \"optional\" ? \"Optional idea\" : activity.flexibility === \"fixed\" ? \"Fixed anchor\" : \"Selected · flexible\"}</span></div>\n+                <h3>{activity.title}</h3>\n+                <FormattedNotes markdown={activity.notes_markdown} />\n+                {activity.booking && <aside className=\"booking\" aria-label=\"Booking and evidence\">\n+                  <strong>Supplier record: {activity.booking.supplier_status} · synthetic</strong>\n+                  <p>{activity.booking.supplier} · {activity.booking.local_start.slice(0, 5)} {activity.booking.timezone}</p>\n+                  <p><span className=\"evidence-label\">Evidence unverified</span> · {activity.booking.evidence.source}</p>\n+                  <p>{activity.booking.evidence.uncertainty}</p>\n+                </aside>}\n+                <div className=\"activity-actions\">\n+                  <button disabled={pending || !!editing} onClick={() => setEditing({ activity, revision: day.revision })}>Edit title & notes</button>\n+                  <div className=\"reorder\" role=\"group\" aria-label={`Reorder ${activity.title}`}>\n+                    <button disabled={pending || index === 0 || !!editing} aria-label={`Move ${activity.title} up`} onClick={() => {\n+ <skill_content name="web-interface-review">
# Skill: web-interface-review

# Web Interface Review

## Job and boundary
Find observable interface problems that could confuse, exclude, or mislead real users. Review the named screens or changed flows, not the entire application by default. This is distinct from `frontend-design` (creating visual direction), `react-nextjs-practices` (framework implementation), and `code-review-quality` (general diff review).

## Review
1. Ground the review in the target users, approved behavior, actual code, and available running interface. Confirm which states and viewports can be inspected. If runtime access is missing, mark findings code-based rather than claiming tested behavior.
2. Check the relevant fundamentals: clear labels and purpose, hierarchy, keyboard/focus support, screen-reader semantics where inspectable, sufficient contrast, accessible feedback, touch targets, responsive layout, navigation, reduced motion, and meaningful loading/empty/error/success states.
3. For changed mutations, ask whether the UI honestly represents pending, saved, rejected, and stale/conflicting states. Client confirmation must not imply that a backend or external booking succeeded when it did not.
4. When current, authoritative [Vercel Web Interface Guidelines](https://github.com/vercel-labs/web-interface-guidelines) are accessible, consult applicable rules; do not let external fetch failure prevent a bounded local review. Avoid treating a checklist as automated proof of accessibility.
5. Report only actionable, evidenced issues with affected route/component, impact, severity, and a minimal corrective direction. Distinguish definite problems, likely problems needing runtime inspection, and subjective preferences. Do not silently edit when the request is review-only.

## Stop condition
The review covers the affected user flows with honest evidence levels and a prioritized, nonduplicated set of findings (or a grounded no-issue conclusion). No mandatory report for unaffected pages.

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\tanch\.config\opencode\skills\web-interface-review
Relative paths in this skill (e.g., scripts/, reference/) are relative to this base directory.

<skill_content name="code-review-quality">
# Skill: code-review-quality

# Code Review and Quality

## Job and boundary
Assess whether a specific change improves the software safely. Review-only intent never authorizes edits, commits, host review submission, merges, or deployment. This skill reviews implementation quality; `git-branch-to-review` owns managed branch/PR delivery and merge permission; `code-design` owns a requested structural design job. The author's own review is not an independent audit.

## Review the evidence
1. Read the objective, affected contracts, repository conventions, changed files, and diff against the actual target. Look first at tests and observable behavior claimed by the change; do not infer success from a green status without examining what it checks.
2. Follow important changed execution paths, including error/empty/stale/concurrent cases. Apply the relevant axes proportionately:
   - Correctness: requirements, invariants, edge cases, state, transactions, and effective tests.
   - Readability: changes another developer could follow; avoid preference-only criticism.
   - Architecture: ownership and coupling with demonstrated change burden.
   - Security: changed trust boundaries and privilege/data exposure.
   - Performance: credible workload or observed regression, not speculative micro-optimizations.
3. Confirm whether mandatory checks actually ran on the reviewed revision. If absent, report missing evidence rather than inventing failures or assuming they pass. Suggest the smallest decisive test when helpful.
4. Findings should identify exact file/line or path when available, a concrete failure scenario, severity/blocking status, and the minimal remedy. Prioritize real defects over exhaustive stylistic checklists. It is valid to find no meaningful issues.
5. Give a merge/revise recommendation tied to evidence and remaining risks. A review recommendation is not a merge or a permission to fix the issues. If review triggers discovery of a material new threat or contract design question, point to the appropriate specialist without launching a mandatory skill chain.

## Stop condition
A reviewer can see the significant findings, their evidence and severity, actual check status, and a proportionate recommendation. Do not require every axis to have a finding or spawn subagents without explicit approval.

## Source
Adapted from [Addy Osmani's Code Review and Quality](https://github.com/addyosmani/agent-skills/tree/main/skills/code-review-and-quality), focusing on concrete findings rather than an always-exhaustive ceremony.
